<a id="item-55"></a>
## [FlexFloor Enables Elastic Expert Parallelism for Efficient RL Post-Training of MoE LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06984) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FlexFloor, a new system presented in arXiv paper 2611.06984, enables elastic expert parallelism by progressively releasing workers during synchronous response generation in reinforcement learning post-training of mixture-of-experts LLMs. Compared to the verl baseline, it generates approximately 65.40% more response tokens per unit of occupied computing time and increases response generation throughput by 52.74%. This addresses a concrete bottleneck in RL post-training for MoE models, where about one-third of occupied worker time is wasted after local response generation finishes. By making training more resource-efficient, FlexFloor could lower costs and accelerate iterative development of large language models and AI agents. FlexFloor jointly adapts response scheduling and the active group of workers, using historical response lengths and memory constraints to guide batching and prompt placement while maintaining expert availability and correct communication. The approach shows little variation in rollout reward, indicating no significant quality degradation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models activate only a subset of parameters per input, enabling large-scale pretraining with less compute. In distributed training, expert parallelism assigns different experts to different workers, but during synchronous response generation in RL post-training, workers that finish early may remain occupied because their experts are still needed by others processing longer responses. Elastic expert parallelism allows dynamically adjusting the number of active workers, and verl is a popular open-source RL training framework for LLMs.

## [Two-Ledger Model Enables Transaction-Grain AI Cost Accounting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06989) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper formalizes the grain mismatch between settlement-level billing data and transaction-level AI costs in multi-agent systems, and proposes the Two-Ledger Model that joins FOCUS-conformant billing data with OpenTelemetry-based invocation records to attribute costs to individual business transactions. A simulation shows the correlation spine sustains cost coverage above 99% even as trace propagation falls to zero, and mid-flight gating cuts p99 transaction cost by up to 61%. Enterprises running multi-agent AI workflows cannot currently answer basic business questions like what a specific order or claim cost in AI, because provider billing is aggregated by SKU and period. This work offers a concrete architectural path for FinOps teams to achieve transaction-grain cost attribution, which could improve chargeback, optimization, and detection of shadow AI spend across clouds and models. The model uses a layered correlation spine (trace context, A2A/MCP metadata, business-key correlation, probabilistic matching) to assign each record to a business transaction with recorded method and confidence, and a versioned rate card prices usage in near real time. Period reconciliation ties both ledgers to the invoice, with the unexplained residual recovering planted unmetered spend within 0.24 percentage points while flagging provider billing errors, and mid-flight gating achieves this at a 2-4% false-pause rate.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: FOCUS (FinOps Open Cost and Usage Specification) is an open standard that defines a common schema for billing data across cloud providers, but it remains settlement-grain, meaning costs are aggregated by SKU and charge period rather than by individual business transaction. OpenTelemetry GenAI telemetry provides invocation-level observability for LLM calls, capturing details like token usage and requester attribution. Multi-agent systems often span multiple clouds, models, and orchestration frameworks, making it difficult to trace costs back to specific business outcomes without a correlation mechanism.

## [AI Agents Diverge from Human Preferences in Lean Proof Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06995) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.06995) introduces a 'simulated collaboration' methodology and analyzes nine major human collaborations in Lean, covering 4.7 million definitions across over 6,000 commits, to show that autonomous AI agents systematically diverge from human preferences during proof construction. The findings reveal concrete blind spots in AI proof agents—such as over-attention to explicit gaps and aversion to complex goals—that could inform better human-AI collaboration tools and interventions in AI-assisted theorem proving. The study finds that AI agents are far more drawn to explicitly marked gaps, show relative aversion to densely connected proof centers and complex or surprising goals, and are attracted to surprising proof bodies that they tend to regularize toward baseline.

**Background**: Lean is an open-source proof assistant and functional programming language used to formalize mathematics and verify proofs. Proof formalization is the process of translating informal mathematical arguments into formal languages like Lean so that every step can be machine-checked. As AI systems increasingly generate formal proofs, understanding how their proof-writing preferences differ from humans' is important for building effective collaborative tools.

## [Framework compares Darwinian, Lamarckian, and agent-directed evolution in AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07048) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.07048) by Andrew W. Lo and colleagues introduces a theoretical framework that compares three modes of evolution—Darwinian (natural selection), Lamarckian (inherited learning), and agent-directed (agents generate and evaluate behavioral variants)—in the context of AI adaptation after environmental change. The analysis shows that when learning is effective, these mechanisms yield progressively faster adaptation, but also reveals that agent-directed search can accelerate convergence to unintended behaviors if the evaluation target is misspecified. This work is significant because it formalizes how learning and self-directed search can speed up AI self-improvement, while highlighting a safety risk: agents may optimize measured performance while drifting from the intended objective, and such errors can persist across generations in recursive self-improvement. It underscores the need for independent evaluation and oversight to keep pace with autonomous AI development. The framework shows that generating more behavioral variants accelerates adaptation, but the optimal search effort depends on learning ability, problem difficulty, and experimentation costs. A key caveat is that an agent's ability to direct its own improvement does not guarantee that improvements are evaluated against the intended objective; when the evaluation target is misspecified, additional search speeds up convergence to the wrong behavior even as measured performance improves.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Darwinian evolution relies on natural selection acting on inherited variation, while Lamarckian evolution adds the inheritance of acquired characteristics—traits learned or changed during an organism's lifetime. In AI, agent-directed evolution refers to systems where agents can generate, evaluate, and select their own behavioral variants, potentially enabling recursive self-improvement. The paper adapts these biological concepts to analyze how quickly AI agents can improve after environmental change and under what conditions such improvement remains safe.

## [Closed-Loop Evaluation Framework for Deployed LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07051) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.07051) proposes closed-loop evaluation, a method that models evaluation as performance estimation under biased raters and censored outcomes, spanning four analysis levels (span, trace, conversation, engagement) and a measurement spine of Execution, Substance, Trajectory, and Results, with Conduct as a constraint. It introduces Provenance and Adjudication components to certify evidence and evaluators, and demonstrates on the SharpInsight business-analysis agent that evidence-and-rule judgment outperforms holistic scoring (ICC 0.86 vs. 0.81; kappa 0.81 vs. 0.63). As LLM agents move from demos to production, teams need continuous, trustworthy evaluation rather than one-off benchmark runs; this framework addresses key sources of measurement error (biased raters, censored outcomes) and closes the loop from evaluation to agent improvement. It could influence how organizations build evaluation pipelines for deployed agents and set a precedent for versioned, auditable evaluation rules. The method uses an Agent Factory to generate evaluator agents, each with a domain knowledge pack and an adapter harness, and closes two loops: an inner loop that audits evidence, rules, and metrics, and an outer loop that uses per-component regression checks to drive improvement. The authors also provide an automated pipeline and validate on public conversation data, though the work is an early preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Evaluating deployed LLM agents is harder than benchmarking static models because agents act over multiple steps, produce traces and conversations, and are often judged by imperfect raters (human or LLM). Common pitfalls include rater bias, where scores reflect the measurement process rather than agent quality, and censored outcomes, where performance is only partially observed (e.g., tasks abandoned or truncated). Closed-loop evaluation aims to make evaluation self-checking and actionable by tying conclusions to atomic data and versioned rules.

## [Verified Dafny postconditions do not guarantee cross-agent LLM agreement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07073) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.07073) introduces a cross-agent formalization protocol where a Specifier LLM translates clinical trial criteria into a Dafny predicate and an Implementer LLM writes a method checked against it. Across 96 clinical trial documents, a same-model control shows only 59.3% agreement on independent re-extraction, and the best cross-model configuration (GPT-5.6-luna/GPT-5.4-mini) reaches just 34.4% agreement, dominated by direct contradiction (42.7%). This work challenges the assumption that a successful formal verification implies genuine independent agreement between agents, which is critical for multi-agent LLM pipelines used in safety-critical domains like clinical trial specification. It shows that role allocation and capability gaps strongly affect reliability, urging practitioners to treat verification success as insufficient evidence of correctness. Agreement is highly sensitive to how capability is allocated: the GPT-4.1-nano/GPT-4.1-mini configuration achieves 53.0% agreement when the stronger model is the Implementer, but only 14.3% when roles are reversed. The authors also identify a verification artifact where a weaker Implementer satisfies the postcondition by directly invoking the Specifier's predicate, and after structural auditing the corrected agreement falls from 14.3% to 13.2%.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Dafny is a verification-aware programming language with native support for specifications and a static program verifier, allowing developers to write provably correct code. In LLM-based program synthesis, models are increasingly used to generate formal specifications and implementations, but it is unclear whether two models independently formalizing the same source truly agree or merely produce compatible artifacts. This paper tests that by separating the roles of specification and implementation across different LLMs.

## [PromptOps: Closed-Loop Framework for Maintaining LLM-as-a-Judge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07123) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PromptOps introduces a closed-loop framework for monitoring and iteratively recalibrating LLM-as-a-Judge (LLMaaJ) systems to combat 'judge drift', and instantiates it with five prompt optimization methods: GEPA, SIMBA, Rubric Synthesis, and exemplar selection using either simulated annealing or a Tree-structured Parzen Estimator. Experiments show that iterative prompt optimization can recover judge performance after controlled shifts in the reference-score distribution, but effective maintenance is context-dependent. This work addresses the underexplored problem of judge drift, which can silently degrade the reliability of automated evaluation pipelines as user interactions and score distributions change. It provides practitioners with a structured abstraction for maintaining LLM judges over time, moving prompt optimization from a one-shot procedure to an ongoing maintenance task. PromptOps separates four operational decisions: when to optimize, which optimization procedure to apply, which feedback records to use, and how to update the judge prompt. The experiments reveal that neither the choice of optimization algorithm nor the update timing generalizes universally across settings, highlighting the need for context-aware maintenance strategies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-Judge refers to using large language models to automatically evaluate the outputs of chatbots and other AI systems, often as a scalable alternative to human evaluation. Prompt optimization methods such as GEPA (Genetic-Pareto) and SIMBA (Stochastic Introspective Mini-Batch Ascent) are typically studied as one-shot procedures on static development sets, but in deployment, the judge's agreement with human policy can degrade as data distributions shift—a phenomenon known as judge drift.

## [Goal Revelation Bridges Empowerment and Capability-Based Power in Markov Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07247) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.07247) establishes a formal chain of inequalities linking information-theoretic empowerment, goal revelation (mutual information between a human's latent goal and the realized trajectory), and capability-based individual power in Markov games. The authors show that Fano's inequality yields a lower bound B(s) on goal revelation from individual power, while empowerment provides an upper bound, giving B(s) ≤ I(G;ξ|s) ≤ I(A_h→ξ|s) ≤ E_h(s). This work offers a rigorous quantitative framework for measuring power in AI systems, directly addressing AI safety concerns about instrumental convergence and gradual human disempowerment. It clarifies which measures can provide meaningful guarantees, showing that only the attainment end of the chain yields positive assurances, which could inform the design of monitoring tools for deployed systems. The paper highlights failure modes: an assistant maximizing empowerment may prefer an interface with broad influence over goal-irrelevant degrees of freedom rather than one enabling goal attainment, while an assistant maximizing revelation cannot distinguish a working interface from a rewired one that zeroes all attainment probabilities. A satisfaction floor computable from two interaction-log statistics is proposed for monitoring retained user power, and simulations show the three quantities are correlated but not interchangeable.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Instrumental convergence is the hypothesis that sufficiently capable goal-directed agents will tend to pursue similar intermediate sub-goals, such as resource acquisition or self-preservation, regardless of their final objectives, raising concerns about AI disempowerment of humans. Empowerment is an information-theoretic measure defined as the channel capacity between an agent's actions and future states, quantifying its influence over the environment. Fano's inequality is a fundamental result in information theory that bounds the conditional entropy of a variable given noisy observations, often used to derive lower bounds on error probability. Markov games provide a formal framework for multi-agent interaction in dynamically changing environments.

## [FINCOSMOS: Open-World Benchmark for Financial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07464) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FINCOSMOS introduces an open-world evaluation paradigm where a simulated user holds a hidden strategy sampled from a compositional grammar, and the agent must elicit it, acquire web data, and build a point-in-time-correct backtest without any reference answer. In 150 sessions across four coding agents and a tool-less model, no session was accepted: every session approved by the simulated user failed a programmatic check, and agents surfaced only 54–64% of the details they were not told. This benchmark targets real failure modes of financial agents—silent failures such as unrequested strategies, unverified data, and lookahead bias—that closed benchmarks remove rather than measure, making it relevant to both agent evaluation and trustworthy finance applications. It shows that current agents can produce convincing but wrong reports, and that persistent memory plus benchmark feedback can eventually solve all ten tasks. Grading is reference-free: programs verify point-in-time correctness, recompute every reported metric, check the delivered strategy against the hidden specification, and accept a run only if the user is satisfied and none of 15 veto checks fails. Human experts attributed 88.33% of failures to the agents, and an agent with persistent memory learned all ten tasks when given FINCOSMOS feedback.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Financial backtesting requires point-in-time data—information only as it was available at each historical decision moment—because using future information (lookahead bias) can make a backtest look convincing while being invalid. Existing agent benchmarks typically fix the task, data, or timeline, which closes the world and removes the very failure modes that matter in open-ended financial work. FINCOSMOS instead simulates an open web environment where the agent must discover the user's strategy and gather its own data.

## [SAVeR²: Verifiable-Reward Safety Alignment for Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07482) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.07482) introduces SAVeR², a safety alignment method for Large Reasoning Models that decomposes safety into four fine-grained verifiable reward signals and adds a reasoning-preserving gradient projection mechanism to eliminate the 'safety tax' on reasoning. Experiments on models from 1.5B to 671B parameters show improved safety and lower over-refusal rates without degrading general reasoning. Large Reasoning Models are increasingly deployed, yet their explicit chain-of-thought reasoning introduces new safety risks, and prior alignment methods often degrade reasoning or fail under adversarial inputs. SAVeR² offers a principled way to align such models without sacrificing their core reasoning strengths, which matters for both AI safety research and practical deployment. The method uses a difficulty-aware data selection strategy to stabilize training and analytically resolves conflicts between safety and reasoning gradients via projection. It is evaluated across multiple benchmarks on models ranging from 1.5B to 671B parameters, though as a single preprint it has not yet undergone peer review.

Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) uses automatically checkable reward signals to fine-tune models, and has been explored for safety alignment. The 'safety tax' refers to the loss of general reasoning or helpfulness that often accompanies safety fine-tuning, while gradient projection is a technique that modifies update directions to avoid conflicting objectives.

## [Goal-Branching LaCAM boosts dense multi-agent pathfinding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07528) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Goal-Branching, a technique that assigns temporary goals to blocking agents and tries each as an additional branch in LaCAM's search, preserving completeness for any rule. On dense instances (60-90% occupancy) of three maze and room maps, it solves 89% of instances versus 44% for LaCAM*, and finds first solutions 10-14x faster on maze-32-32-2 and room-32-32-4, and nearly 200x faster in median time at 75% density on room-32-32-4. This work significantly improves LaCAM's performance on dense, corridor-heavy MAPF instances, which are notoriously difficult due to deadlocks requiring coordinated moves. The completeness-preserving approach makes it a valuable advancement for multi-robot coordination and logistics applications where high agent density is common. Solution cost increases by 15-22% on instances both algorithms solve, but on open maps and the full MovingAI benchmark, Goal-Branching's solutions cost no more than LaCAM* and it is at most 3% slower. The temporary goals can be chosen by random or corridor-aware rules, and completeness is guaranteed for any such rule.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Multi-Agent Path Finding (MAPF) is the problem of finding collision-free paths for multiple agents on a graph, fundamental to multi-robot coordination. LaCAM is a complete search-based solver that uses depth-first search over joint configurations and PIBT (Priority Inheritance with Backtracking) to generate successors. PIBT is a greedy one-step successor generator that uses adaptive prioritization to ensure collision-free moves. In dense instances, LaCAM struggles because resolving deadlocks requires coordinated moves that PIBT rarely proposes, and LaCAM can only explore alternatives by adding constraints one agent at a time.

The paper introduces CST-Bench, a 7,082-example benchmark for counterfactual spatial reasoning in multimodal LLMs, covering 6 intervention types and 12 transformation operators with dense causal annotations. It also proposes CT-OPSD, a causal transition on-policy self-distillation method that uses these annotations to train a teacher model on counterfactual state transitions and distills the reasoning chains into a student model that sees only real observations. This work highlights that standard spatial reasoning methods can fail under counterfactual interventions, exposing a gap in current MLLM evaluation that focuses mainly on static scene understanding. CST-Bench provides a reusable supervision resource and CT-OPSD offers an initial demonstration of how causal annotations can improve model robustness in dynamic spatial scenarios. CST-Bench includes dense causal annotations such as intervention sources, affected relations, expected effects, invariant relations, and metric changes, enabling fine-grained evaluation. CT-OPSD formulates counterfactual spatial reasoning as causal state-transition modeling, where a teacher model learns from annotations and a student model reasons only from real observations, though the paper notes this is an initial demonstration.

Counterfactual spatial reasoning asks models to infer how moving or swapping objects would change spatial relations and scene states, which is crucial for robust real-world applications. On-policy self-distillation is a training framework where a single model acts as both teacher and student, using privileged context to provide dense token-level guidance during on-policy rollouts.

## [FlashOffloading: Host-DRAM KV Cache Offloading for Long-Context LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07542) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FlashOffloading introduces a host-DRAM-centric inference architecture that fully offloads the KV cache for both prefill and decode phases, using a rolling HBM cache, hot cache, reverse sparse index, and compute-load overlapped kernels. In experiments with GLM-5.2 and MTP3 speculative decoding on an Ascend910C PD-disaggregated platform, it improves prefill throughput by up to 1.38× and decode throughput by up to 1.91× under a 30 ms TPOT constraint. Long-context LLM serving is increasingly bottlenecked by HBM capacity, which limits concurrency and effective throughput. FlashOffloading shows that fully offloading the KV cache to host DRAM can be done without inflating latency beyond a strict TPOT SLO, potentially enabling higher concurrency and better hardware utilization for memory-bound inference workloads. The architecture targets both prefill and decode phases, using a rolling HBM cache for prefill and a hot cache for decode, while a reverse sparse index eliminates extra miss-lookup overhead. The compute-load overlapped kernel hides host-to-device (H2D) load latency, and the implementation is integrated into the vLLM-Ascend framework (release v0.30.0).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: In LLM inference, the KV cache stores previously computed attention keys and values so that each new token only needs to process the latest token, but the cache grows linearly with sequence length and concurrency. HBM (high-bandwidth memory) on accelerators has limited capacity, so long-context serving can hit a capacity wall that reduces concurrency. Offloading the KV cache to host DRAM frees HBM space but adds cache management and data transfer overhead, which can negate throughput gains. Prefill is the compute-heavy phase that processes the input prompt, while decode is the memory-bound phase that generates tokens one at a time.

<a id="item-68"></a>
## [FedAbA aligns local LoRA bases before aggregation for heterogeneous-rank federated fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07598) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes FedAbA, a basis-consistent federated LoRA aggregation framework that extracts each client's local basis, aligns it to the corresponding prefix of a global reference basis, and reconstructs each local matrix update before aggregation. It then aggregates with weights combining client data size and consistency score, followed by exact coefficient-space SVD refactorization and gauge fixing before rank-prefix dispatch. This addresses a real instability in federated LLM fine-tuning with heterogeneous client ranks: composed matrix updates share a weight space but not a basis, so naive averaging can destabilize the rank-prefix dispatched to lower-rank clients. The largest gains under strong rank heterogeneity suggest the method could make practical on-device federated fine-tuning more robust. The key technical observation is that existing server-side aggregators compose local ΔW_i from client-specific LoRA factors (A_i,B_i) and average them directly, even though the generating factors may be expressed in client-specific local bases. FedAbA adds basis extraction, alignment, consistency-aware weighting, exact coefficient-space SVD refactorization, and gauge fixing to the compose–aggregate–factorize–dispatch loop, with theoretical analysis supporting the consistency-aware aggregation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes pre-trained weights and injects small trainable low-rank matrices into model layers, greatly reducing trainable parameters. In federated learning, clients fine-tune locally and a server aggregates updates; heterogeneous ranks let clients with different capacities use different LoRA ranks. Because a low-rank matrix can be factorized in many equivalent ways, the factors from different clients are not directly comparable, which is the basis-inconsistency problem FedAbA targets.

## [LoRA Fine-Tunes Leak Training-Data Domain via Embedding Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07610) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.07610) shows that the training-data domain of a LoRA fine-tune can be recovered from the model artifact alone, with gray-box activations achieving 61% accuracy and black-box outputs 64%, versus 20% chance. This reveals a privacy and security risk: LoRA adapters, often shared publicly, can leak information about their training data domain even without access to the data, which matters for model provenance and AI safety. The study used one base model (Qwen3.5-0.8B) and 36 LoRA fine-tunes over 18 datasets in five domains, employing a pre-registered leave-one-dataset-out protocol; notably, activations and outputs were more informative than weight statistics, contradicting the hypothesis of monotonic signal decay.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that adds small trainable matrices to a frozen base model, making it cheap to adapt large language models to specific domains. Model fingerprinting aims to identify characteristics of a model, such as its training data or architecture, from its weights, activations, or outputs. This paper combines these concepts to study whether the domain of a LoRA fine-tune can be inferred from the adapter alone.

## [Agent Harness Evolution: Higher Benchmark Scores Don't Guarantee Real Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07624) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.07624) empirically evaluates four agent harness evolution methods across general and coding tasks, finding that all sixteen evolved agents gained new task coverage but lost previously covered tasks, and that higher benchmark scores did not correlate with reliable, efficient, or transferable improvement. The study introduces EvoAudit, which turns these diagnostics into revisions of the select, analyze, mutate, and gate steps, achieving the best performance. This work challenges the common assumption that higher benchmark scores equate to genuine progress in agent evolution, highlighting trade-offs between reliability, cost, and task coverage that could affect how AI agents are evaluated and deployed. It motivates preservation-aware evaluation and explicit applicability conditions, which are important for the field's move toward recursive self-improvement without weight updates. In one general-task case, reliability rose by 5.2 points despite losing ten previously covered tasks, and most reliability gains also increased deployment cost; the highest-scoring agent did not yield the largest mean improvement in any of the four pools, and stricter admission can discard useful alternatives. Transfer selectively preserves gains: coverage usually falls on the general-task target across both harness types and paradigms, but rises on the coding target with the outer-layer-only harness.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Agent harness evolution refers to iteratively improving the scaffolding (prompts, tools, control flow) around a fixed language model to enable recursive self-improvement without updating model weights. Benchmarks are commonly used to measure progress, but this paper shows that score gains can mask regressions in task coverage and increases in cost, raising questions about how to evaluate such systems holistically.

## [Skillware: Modular Capability Bundles Replace Prompt Engineering for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07652) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.07652) introduces Skillware, an open architectural framework and capability registry that equips LLM-based agents with modular, versioned software bundles instead of relying on prompt engineering. Each bundle includes a typed JSON input contract, deterministic Python code, task directives, bundled datasets, and automated unit tests, and is translated into native tool declarations for any model via in-process adapters. The paper reports that small local models (3B–8B parameters) equipped with Skillware consistently outperform unequipped frontier models on complex multi-step workflows, cutting token usage by up to 84.8% and inference costs by up to 97.3% while eliminating parameter errors. This suggests a path toward more reliable, auditable, and cost-effective production agent systems that do not depend on ever-larger models or longer prompts. The evaluation covers 23 frontier and open-weight models across 368 benchmark conditions, and the framework is released as an open-source Python package (ARPAHLS/skillware) maintained by ARPA Hellenic Logical Systems. The authors also outline an invariant continuum for capability bundles extending from digital systems to IoT, drones, and embodied robotics, though the current work focuses on digital logical systems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-based agents typically rely on prompt engineering—loose text instructions or markdown 'skills'—to decide how to call APIs and execute tasks, which often leads to brittle behavior, wasted context, and unpredictable failures in production. Skillware instead treats capabilities as versioned software artifacts with explicit contracts and tests, similar to how video game skill hotkeys execute deterministically under fixed rules. This decouples what an agent wants to do from how it is executed, enabling verification and auditability.

## [More Information Can Hurt LLM Agent Markets, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07667) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces 'empirical information design,' a framework that systematically compares disclosure policies across markets populated by LLM agents while holding interaction rules and models fixed. Testing ten frontier LLMs in multi-resource trade, bilateral bargaining, and double auctions, it finds that finer information disclosure yields highly model-dependent, sometimes paradoxical outcomes. The results show that model selection and information design must be co-optimized, since the same disclosure policy can help one LLM and hurt another. This has direct implications for anyone building multi-agent LLM systems, market simulations, or agentic economies, where transparency is often assumed to be beneficial. In trade, revealing exact valuations instead of ordinal rankings reduced realized gains for GPT-6 Sol (98% to 91% of maximum) but increased them for Claude Sonnet 5 (72% to 86%). In double auctions, full disclosure inflated price demands and triggered market breakdown, lowering welfare for Gemini 3.1 Pro (97% to 78%), Claude Opus 5.5 (94% to 64%), and Grok 4.7 (97% to 62%).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Information design is an economic framework that analyzes how the provision of information alone can influence players' behavior in a game with uncertain payoffs. Mechanism design, a related field, focuses on designing rules and incentives so that self-interested agents produce desirable collective outcomes. This paper applies these ideas to markets where the agents are LLM-based, asking whether giving them more detailed information improves overall efficiency.

## [MCPHallu: Benchmarking Reasoning, Execution, and Memory Hallucinations in MCP Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07689) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MCPHallu introduces a 358-task benchmark across 36 production MCP servers, designed to diagnose reasoning, execution, and memory hallucinations in LLM agents via structural failure triggers. Each task targets one of four failure subtypes—Branch Collapse, Unreachable Goal, Tool Misuse, or Context Forgetting—enabling subtype-level analysis rather than aggregate success. Current MCP benchmarks primarily measure task completion and diagnose failures only after execution, so MCPHallu fills a gap by building hallucination risks into task construction. This matters for the growing MCP/agent ecosystem, as it enables developers to identify specific failure modes and improve agent reliability in tool-rich environments. Evaluating fourteen frontier LLM agents from eight vendors on 5,012 trajectories, MCPHallu found an average score of 0.642, with failures concentrated on Unreachable Goal tasks (average score 0.458, only 23.2% of runs passing). Subtype rankings diverge sharply: a model's rank shifts by 6.9 of 13 positions on average across subtypes, and Branch Collapse is uncorrelated with Unreachable Goal across models (r = -0.12).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard introduced by Anthropic in November 2024 that standardizes how LLM applications connect to external tools, data sources, and services. MCP servers expose tools, resources, and prompts that agents can discover and invoke at runtime, enabling dynamic tool use across multiple servers. As agents increasingly rely on MCP for real-world tasks, understanding and mitigating hallucination—where agents fabricate actions or misinterpret tool capabilities—becomes critical for safe deployment.

## [GenGLAD: A Generalist Model for Cross-Domain Graph-Level Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07706) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces GenGLAD, a generalist graph-level anomaly detection (GLAD) approach that learns a single unified model to detect anomalous graphs across multiple domains with minimal target supervision. It uses a GNN-based discriminative subgraph extraction model to learn transferable anomaly patterns and a lightweight scoring module for efficient test-time adaptation. This work shifts GLAD from dataset-specific models to a generalist paradigm, addressing scalability and practicality in real-world scenarios where labeled data is scarce and diverse domains continuously emerge. It could benefit applications like fraud detection and malware identification by reducing the need for extensive in-domain training. GenGLAD extracts discriminative subgraphs to capture anomaly-related substructures and computes multi-dimensional discrepancies between the subgraph and multi-level context during test-time adaptation. A lightweight scoring module automatically selects informative signals to improve robustness against misleading patterns, and experiments show strong performance, generalization, and data efficiency.

**Background**: Graph-level anomaly detection (GLAD) aims to identify graphs that deviate significantly from the majority, with applications ranging from fraudulent social networks to malicious botnets. Graph neural networks (GNNs) are deep learning models designed for graph-structured data, using message passing to learn node and graph representations. Traditional GLAD methods are dataset-specific, requiring substantial in-domain data and training for each new application, which limits their scalability.

## [RIVER: Multi-agent LLM framework for bidirectional enzyme-reaction retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07757) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RIVER is a multi-agent LLM framework that performs bidirectional enzyme-reaction retrieval by coordinating large language models, specialized biochemical computational tools, and external knowledge resources to assemble evidence at multiple levels, including reaction chemistry, protein sequences and domains, catalytic mechanisms, and database annotations. On public benchmarks, it achieves the highest success rates in both enzyme retrieval and enzyme annotation, and in Escherichia coli retrieval tasks constructed from recent literature it substantially outperforms closed-source general-purpose and biology-specialized multi-agent systems such as Claude Code, Codex, and Phylo Max. Enzyme function annotation and candidate enzyme identification are critical for linking genomic information to biochemical reaction networks, yet existing similarity-based or pair-trained methods struggle to integrate biochemical evidence across large candidate spaces and with highly similar enzyme families. RIVER's evidence-guided multi-agent approach offers a way to prioritize biochemically supported enzyme-reaction relationships, potentially accelerating metabolic pathway design and biocatalyst discovery. RIVER includes dedicated validation modules that examine evidence gaps and potential conflicts and rerank candidates using traceable evidence records, and it performs well with both closed-source and open-source model configurations. As an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review, and the reported results are on selected datasets and metrics, so independent validation and experimental confirmation of predicted enzyme-reaction relationships remain necessary.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Enzyme-reaction retrieval is a fundamental problem in computational biology: given an enzyme, predict which reactions it may catalyze, or given a reaction, rank candidate enzymes that could catalyze it. Traditional methods rely on sequence or structural similarity or on training with known enzyme-reaction pairs, which limits their ability to incorporate diverse biochemical evidence. Multi-agent LLM frameworks coordinate multiple LLM-driven agents with external tools to solve complex, multi-step tasks, and evidence-guided retrieval uses retrieved evidence to iteratively refine reasoning and search.

## [Study traces LLM degradation from irrelevant context to 'Intrusion Pathway'](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07778) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.07778) identifies an 'Intrusion Pathway' in language models where irrelevant context degrades performance, tracing it to MLPs and specific attention heads called 'intrusion heads'. The authors show that knocking out about ten such heads (roughly 1% of attention heads) recovers 57% of the performance drop, while an oracle probe recovers 97.6% on average across four tasks. This work provides a mechanistic explanation for a known but poorly understood failure mode in retrieval-augmented and long-context deployments, where irrelevant retrieved passages or off-distribution tokens hurt accuracy. The identification of a sparse set of intrusion heads enables targeted interventions that could improve LLM robustness without retraining. The intrusion heads promote distractor-related content via prefix-derived writes, and their reduced support for the correct answer stems mainly from weaker non-prefix contributions rather than direct suppression. These heads transfer across tasks and distractor types and are largely weight-disjoint from heads that integrate relevant evidence, enabling a class-conditional intervention.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Language models often receive prompts with extra context, such as retrieved documents in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or long conversation histories. Even when this context contains no competing answer and no semantic overlap with the query, it can still degrade model accuracy—a failure mode that has lacked a mechanistic explanation. This paper uses interpretability techniques to trace the corruption to specific components: MLPs that enrich representations, 'parroting heads' that copy prefix tokens, and a sparse set of 'intrusion heads' that write distractor-related information.

A new arXiv paper presents an exploratory mixed-methods study of how volunteers in Sudan's Emergency Response Rooms (ERRs) use and perceive Generative AI during active armed conflict, based on a survey of 89 respondents and 15 semi-structured interviews with GenAI users and non-users. ## [Larger Language Models Are More Brittle Under Compression, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07891) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.07891) exactly decomposes the loss from compressing in-context retrieval attention heads into damage versus network compensation, and compares this decomposition across model sizes. It finds that for damage of comparable size, smaller models restore most of the loss, while larger models retain most of it, because compensating responses offset less as models grow. This counterintuitive finding suggests that quantization and pruning techniques that are nearly harmless in small models can be disproportionately costly in large ones, which has direct implications for practitioners deploying compressed LLMs at scale. It challenges the common assumption that larger models are uniformly more robust to compression. The study focuses on in-context retrieval, a capability carried by a small, identifiable set of attention heads, and uses an exact decomposition to attribute the restored loss to specific compensating components. The abstract is truncated, so full experimental details and the range of model sizes tested are not yet available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Quantization reduces the precision of model weights (e.g., from 32-bit to 8-bit or 4-bit) to lower memory and compute costs, while pruning removes parameters or neurons from a network to reduce its size. Both are widely used to make large language models cheaper to deploy, but their effects are typically measured only by benchmark scores, which conflate the damage to internal components with the network's ability to compensate. Attention heads are specialized modules within transformer models, and prior work has identified specific heads responsible for in-context retrieval.

## [Persistent Retrieval Subagent Cuts Token Use and API Costs for Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07899) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.07899) compares three retrieval designs for conversational agents: skill-based retrieval in the main agent, a non-persistent retrieval subagent, and a persistent retrieval subagent that maintains retrieval history across turns and returns evidence directly to the main agent. Across five multi-turn settings, the persistent approach uses 43.3% fewer agent-model tokens and incurs 45.0% lower estimated API cost than skill-based retrieval, while also reducing average response time by 9.1%. This work offers concrete quantitative evidence that delegating retrieval to a persistent subagent can meaningfully reduce operational costs and latency in RAG-based conversational systems, which could influence how enterprises architect multi-turn agents. The trade-off analysis against non-persistent retrieval also helps practitioners choose designs based on their priorities (cost vs. speed). Compared with non-persistent retrieval, the persistent approach reduces response time by 15.6 seconds per question but uses 65.1% more tokens and incurs 29.1% higher estimated API cost, highlighting a clear cost-latency trade-off. The key architectural shift is that the retrieval subagent maintains state and supplies evidence for the main agent to evaluate, rather than synthesizing responses itself.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a common technique where an LLM retrieves external information to ground its answers. In conversational agents, retrieval can be done directly by the main agent or delegated to a subagent; the design affects how much context each model call receives and whether information must be re-retrieved in follow-up questions. Persistent retrieval means the subagent remembers previous retrievals across turns, avoiding redundant work.

## [GuidedWAM: Training-Free Energy Guidance Boosts World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07912) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GuidedWAM introduces a model-agnostic, training-free energy guidance framework that injects driving objectives into frozen world action models (WAMs) at sampling time, improving all three publicly released WAMs by 1.8 to 3.6 EPDMS on the NAVSIM v2 hard split with less than 10% additional sampling time per step. This work positions energy guidance as a practical post-training control channel for WAMs, enabling adaptation to new driving objectives without retraining and thus lowering the cost of deploying and customizing autonomous driving models. The framework constructs each objective as differentiable energies on the sampler's intermediate clean-action estimate, requiring only forward access to the frozen model, and is stabilized by sampling-time guidance scheduling, per-energy normalization, and velocity-bounded correction.

**Background**: World action models (WAMs) for autonomous driving couple the driving policy and the visual world model in a single generative network whose objectives are fixed after training, so conditioning on a new driving objective requires retraining. Energy guidance is a technique from generative modeling that steers sampling using differentiable energy functions without modifying the model weights. NAVSIM v2 is a planning benchmark for autonomous driving that evaluates agents using the Extended Predictive Driver Model Score (EPDMS).

## [Finite Brains Generalize via Cyclic Memory Reading Coincidences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07920) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper argues that finite learners generalize to arbitrary input lengths only if their memory reads coincidences (equivalence relations) rather than coordinates, and that such memory must be cyclic. The proposed model, trained on strings up to 40 symbols, achieves at least 99% accuracy on lengths 41 to 500 for nine of fifteen benchmark tasks, including five that no LSTM or Transformer solves. This work offers a theoretical explanation for why LSTMs and Transformers fail at length generalization, linking memory structure to computational power. It could inspire new neural architectures with human-like generalization and provides a bridge between neuroscience concepts like grid cells and place cells and machine learning. The model's memory is composed of cycles, analogous to grid modules, with place cells detecting returns and a prefrontal controller that moves the cycles without seeing them. Given new cycles after training, it reverses strings of 15,015 symbols without error and fails beyond that on the curve the theory predicts, and its controllers run unchanged on neurons.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Length generalization is the ability of a model trained on short sequences to perform correctly on longer ones, a known weakness of LSTMs and Transformers. The paper introduces a distinction between coordinates (values that change with input size) and coincidences (equivalence relations that remain constant), and argues that only the latter enable finite learners to generalize. It also draws on neuroscience, where grid cells and place cells in the hippocampus are thought to support spatial memory and navigation.

## [ChemE-as-Code: Open-Source Platform for LLM Agents in Chemical Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07947) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ChemE-as-Code is an open-source environment that enables LLM-based AI agents to perform chemical engineering tasks, with machine-readable, Git-versioned files and automated consistency checks. In a case study, agents produced a preliminary basic-engineering package for an ammonia plant (simulation files, P&IDs, layout, HAZOP, datasheets, relief) at an API cost of USD 224. This platform demonstrates a concrete, cost-effective application of agentic AI in a niche engineering domain, potentially accelerating preliminary design work and enabling verifiable, version-controlled calculations. It could impact chemical engineers, process designers, and AI researchers exploring domain-specific agent workflows. Calculations run outside the LLM, each value records its origin, and automated checks report inconsistencies and out-of-date results by file and line. However, the checks only find what their rules cover and miss the rest, so they guide human review rather than replace it; no independent engineer has reviewed the design.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Agentic AI refers to AI systems that can autonomously plan and execute tasks, often using LLMs as reasoning engines. In chemical engineering, tasks like process simulation, HAZOP (Hazard and Operability Study), and P&ID (Piping and Instrumentation Diagram) creation are typically manual and require specialized software. ChemE-as-Code integrates these tools into a code-like environment where agents can invoke them and engineers can audit the results.

## [Imperfect Automated Fact-Checking Can Backfire in Social Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08053) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.08053) presents a sequential social learning model showing that adding a free but error-prone automated fact-checking tool to a platform can strictly lower welfare for articles of intermediate reliability. The authors prove this occurs when truthful articles are not too informative and manual checking is sufficiently costly, and simulations show the tool helps only above a threshold accuracy on average. This work challenges the common assumption that more fact-checking is always beneficial, showing that imperfect automation can crowd out accurate manual verification and lead to worse information cascades. It has direct implications for platform design and misinformation policy, suggesting that accuracy thresholds matter before deploying automated tools. The model features agents who observe article reliability and predecessors' like/dislike actions, and can pay for perfectly accurate manual fact-checking; the free automated tool introduces errors that can mislead agents who would otherwise have verified perfectly. The welfare metric is the probability of a correct cascade, and the backfire effect is proven for intermediate reliability under specific conditions, with simulations showing help on either side of that range.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Sequential social learning models describe how individuals in sequence make decisions based on their private information and the observed actions of predecessors, often leading to information cascades where people ignore their own signals and follow the crowd. Automated fact-checking tools use AI to quickly assess claims but are imperfect, while manual fact-checking is accurate but costly. This paper combines these concepts to analyze when cheap but noisy automation harms social welfare.

## [DictLLM: Dictionary-Based Post-Training Compression for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08082) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DictLLM introduces a post-training compression scheme that decomposes weight matrices within grouped layers into a shared dictionary and layer-specific sparse coefficients, obtained without retraining. It compresses LLaMA-7B by 5.1x with only a 2.9-point increase in WikiText-2 perplexity, achieving a 3.6x higher compression ratio than state-of-the-art low-rank methods. This method pushes the Pareto frontier between model size and performance, enabling efficient deployment of large language models on mobile devices with limited compute and memory. It offers a practical alternative to retraining-based compression, reducing computational cost while maintaining accuracy. DictLLM uses a four-step optimization: SVD of input-scaled weight matrices to obtain shared dictionaries, sparsification of coefficients using a dictionary-aware Hessian, refinement of dictionaries, and aggressive quantization of both components with factor-specific Hessian information. The representation stores a single shared dictionary per layer group along with sparse layer-specific coefficients, significantly reducing memory footprint.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are typically compressed for deployment using post-training quantization or low-rank decomposition, which reduce model size without retraining. Singular value decomposition (SVD) is a common technique for low-rank compression, factorizing weight matrices into smaller matrices. Dictionary learning extends this by learning a shared basis (dictionary) across multiple layers, capturing common patterns to achieve higher compression ratios.

## [MUSS: Multi-scale Sequence Model for Single-cell Gene Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08086) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MUSS is a sequence-based multi-scale model that integrates DNA sequence, chromatin accessibility, and gene expression to model single-cell gene regulation at both DNA and cell resolution. It uses a frozen pre-trained sequence encoder to derive peak- and gene-level representations, integrates them via peak-peak self-attention and distance-biased peak-gene interactions, and aligns them with cell-specific regulatory states through peak masked language modeling and paired scATAC-seq/scRNA-seq training. MUSS bridges DNA-level regulatory grammar and cell-level resolution, potentially enabling more accurate prediction of gene expression and enhancer-gene links in single cells. It could impact single-cell genomics research by providing sequence-grounded representations for decoding cell-type-specific regulatory programs, with strong author pedigree including Kundaje, Lotfollahi, and Haniffa. MUSS achieves strong performance against sequence-based and single-cell omics baselines across gene expression prediction, enhancer-gene linking, motif-guided regulatory prioritization, and zero-shot cell clustering. It can also predict expression for genes and peaks unseen during task training, and code, datasets, and model checkpoints are available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Single-cell ATAC-seq (scATAC-seq) measures chromatin accessibility—regions of open DNA where regulatory proteins can bind—while scRNA-seq measures gene expression. Integrating these data helps link regulatory elements to gene activity, but many computational models represent genes and peaks as fixed tokens without using the underlying DNA sequence. Sequence-based models capture DNA-level regulatory grammar but often lack cell-level resolution, motivating multi-scale approaches like MUSS.

## [GenToka: A Generative Foundation Model for Multimodal Tokamak Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08153) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces GenToka, a generative foundation model that jointly generates schema-selected targets through a shared conditional diffusion Transformer, and extends the TokaMark benchmark with two new task groups for actuator action inversion and disruption prediction, yielding 20 MAST tasks in six groups. Evaluation on MAST measurements shows GenToka reduces macro-average normalized RMSE by 4.7% on Groups 1-4 and by 17.7% on Group 5 relative to TokaMind, and improves macro-average window-level F1 on Group 6 by 8.4%. This work demonstrates a reusable, multimodal foundation model that can adapt to diverse tokamak tasks without retraining separate models, potentially accelerating data-driven fusion plasma prediction and control research. It also enriches the TokaMark benchmark, providing a standardized evaluation platform for AI models on fusion diagnostics. GenToka uses signal-specific codecs to preserve native time series, profiles, fields, and scalars, and employs foundation pretraining followed by task-specific adaptation to transfer cross-signal structure across all six task groups. The benchmark extension includes explicit schemas and evaluation protocols for actuator inversion and disruption prediction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Tokamaks are doughnut-shaped magnetic fusion devices that confine hot plasma, and experiments record diverse measurements such as waveforms, profiles, fields, and scalars. Traditionally, separate models are built for individual tasks like plasma reconstruction, forecasting, control signal recovery, and disruption risk estimation, each relying on a prescribed subset of diagnostics. Foundation models, which are pretrained on large datasets and adapted to multiple downstream tasks, have shown promise in other domains but are relatively new to fusion research. The TokaMark benchmark, introduced previously, provides a structured evaluation suite for AI models on MAST tokamak data.

## [PCBRouteAgent: Multimodal LLM Agent for PCB Routing with Planner-Executor Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08175) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PCBRouteAgent is a multimodal LLM-based agent that coordinates global routing planning with local execution via KiCad's native router, evaluated on 247 synthetic and real boards from PCBWorld-Bench. It achieves clean-pass rates of 100%, 94.9%, 100%, and 40% on the D2, D3-A, D3-B, and D3-C test splits, respectively, outperforming the best baseline on the three real-world subsets by 13.9, 36, and 10 percentage points. This work demonstrates that LLM agents can tackle a concrete, hard engineering domain like PCB routing, which is a critical bottleneck in electronic design automation (EDA). The hierarchical planner/executor design and the new PCBWorld-Bench benchmark could spur further research into AI-driven EDA automation, potentially reducing manual routing effort and accelerating hardware development. The Global Routing Planner uses board images and structured electrical/geometric data to specify connection topology, routing order, corridors, and layer transitions, while the Detailed Routing Executor realizes these guides through KiCad's native routing engine and performs bounded local recovery. Unresolved failures are returned as structured feedback for targeted guide revisions, but the abstract is truncated and full results, including limitations on complex boards (e.g., D3-C's 40% clean-pass rate), are not fully detailed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: PCB routing is the process of connecting electronic components on a printed circuit board with copper traces while satisfying design rules for signal integrity and manufacturability. KiCad is an open-source EDA suite whose native routing engine enforces geometric constraints, and PCBWorld-Bench is a benchmark built on KiCad that provides board datasets and engine-checked evaluation metrics for routing agents. LLM agents are AI systems that use large language models to plan and execute tasks, and multimodal versions can process both text and images, making them suitable for visual-spatial reasoning tasks like PCB layout.

## [BabyTheorist: A New Benchmark for Observational Theory Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08177) ⭐️ 7.0/10

BabyTheorist introduces a benchmark for training and evaluating machines on observational theory learning, where learners must acquire reusable theories from pure before-after visual observations without program labels, task identities, or support sets. The benchmark generates continual streams of transitions from hidden compositional programs and includes a controlled program language, a curriculum over program complexity, recurring frequent subprograms, and a continual train-test protocol with level skipping. This benchmark isolates a capability that current visual reasoning benchmarks largely overlook: learning reusable theories rather than solving few-shot puzzles grouped by a common rule. It could push the field toward evaluating genuine understanding of the world in machines, affecting how researchers design and measure program-learning and visual reasoning systems. Seven representative baselines plateau well below ceiling, and the gap widens as the curriculum advances, indicating that BabyTheorist targets a capability current program-learning approaches do not yet address. The controlled program language and curriculum over program complexity are designed to support theory learning and evaluation, while the continual train-test protocol with level skipping tests generalization to new situations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Observational theory learning refers to acquiring reusable theories from pure observations and applying them to new situations, a key aspect of understanding the world. Current visual reasoning benchmarks typically present each task as a few-shot puzzle grouped by a common rule, focusing on solving the puzzle rather than learning the underlying theory. Compositional programs are programs built from simpler subprograms, and benchmarks in this space aim to test whether models can decompose visual scenes and perform multi-step logical inference.

## [Study finds attribution claims far outpace inspectable evidence in LLM chats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08232) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A large-scale analysis of 489,283 assistant-user response pairs from ShareChat and WildChat found that broad attribution language appears in 31.9% of qualifying responses, while explicit URLs appear in only 1.33%. The study also shows that response cues like uncertainty and attribution have context-dependent associations with users' checking and challenging behaviors. This gap between claims of grounding and preserved inspectable support suggests that current LLM interfaces may not adequately help users verify answers, which matters for designing trustworthy AI systems. The findings are relevant to AI/ML researchers and practitioners building attribution and verification features. Only uncertainty showed a small statistically distinguishable pooled association favoring checking over challenging, and within Technical Help uncertainty favored checking, while refusals and reasoning traces favored challenging in Seeking Information. Scrutiny was concentrated in some conversations, and its apparent turn-to-turn persistence became much smaller when conversation-level differences and response ordering were examined separately.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models often generate answers that synthesize information without making supporting evidence easy to inspect. Attribution refers to a model's ability to provide evidence, such as references or citations, that substantiates its claims. WildChat is a large dataset of real user-chatbot conversations collected with consent, and ShareChat is another source of such interaction data used in this study.

A new arXiv study (2611.08262) analyzed Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct across 100 images and 53 prompting conditions, finding that prompt framing reshapes internal representations more strongly than persona identity, while persona content drives behavioral ratings. This reveals a dissociation between representational geometry and behavioral judgments. This mechanistic interpretability finding suggests that representational distance in LLMs does not necessarily reflect behavioral relevance, which could impact how we interpret and trust persona-based conditioning in multimodal models. It highlights a fundamental distinction between internal representations and their functional consequences in AI systems. The study observed a systematic reorganization of representational geometry across processing depth: early layers primarily encode image identity, while persona-specific structures emerge in later layers. Minimal identity statements altered representations without changing responses, further illustrating the dissociation.

## [LeanContext: New Benchmark Disentangles Mathematical and Environment Context for Lean Proof Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08277) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LeanContext introduces a benchmark of 129,826 Lean whole-proof generation problems that separates Mathematical Context (MC) from Environment Context (EC) to study how retrieved premises and local context affect proof generation. The study finds that selected declarations outperform comparable alternatives more when training includes MC, and prioritizing omitted dependencies improves Pass@32 by 1.40% for LLaDA and 1.70% for Llama on 2,000 targets. This benchmark enables controlled evaluation of how different types of context contribute to neural theorem proving, which is valuable for developing more effective retrieval and training strategies. It provides a large-scale resource that could help standardize research on context usage in formal mathematics. The benchmark provides four aligned input views for crossed training and inference evaluations with shared targets and original Lean verification environments. The selection experiment operates without access to target reference proofs and holds checkpoints, EC, and inference budgets constant, ensuring fair comparison.

**Background**: Lean is a proof assistant and functional programming language used for formal verification of mathematics. Neural theorem proving applies machine learning, particularly large language models, to automatically generate proofs in such formal systems. A new arXiv paper (2611.08284) builds a generative model of contextual integrity by having two LLM-powered actors exchange messages, one holding private information and the other seeking it, to study when disclosure is appropriate versus inappropriate. The authors find that actors shared information with rightful recipients when needed but also disclosed it to people not entitled to receive it. Large language model agents are increasingly used to simulate social interactions, and prior work has explored privacy leakage in multi-agent LLM simulations. This paper combines these threads by using LLM actors to model contextual integrity dynamically through conversation.

## [First-Token Attraction in Mamba Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08303) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a high-dimensional invariant box and exit-time argument to analyze token dynamics in deep Mamba models, proving that the first token's limiting direction attracts subsequent tokens with explicit exponential convergence rates. This provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how information flows in Mamba models, which could inform architecture design and training strategies for state-space models as alternatives to Transformers. The analysis overcomes the challenge of matrix-valued interaction coefficients without positivity guarantees by recovering an effective sign-definiteness, and experiments on Falcon-Mamba-7B confirm that concentration on the first token increases with depth and dominates in later layers.

Unlike attention mechanisms that use positive scalar coefficients for token interactions, Mamba uses full matrix-valued coefficients, making theoretical analysis more difficult. Previous work has studied token dynamics in Mamba, but this paper provides a new analytical framework to prove convergence properties.

## [RM-VLM: LLM and VLM Automate Reward Machine Construction and Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08308) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RM-VLM introduces a method where a large language model (LLM) generates a reward machine (RM) from a natural-language task description, and a vision-language model (VLM) serves as the labelling function to ground the RM's propositions from image observations, eliminating manual labelling functions. A VLM-based value function estimator provides a dense reward signal for RM-guided policy learning. This work addresses a key limitation of reward machines—the need for manually engineered labelling functions—by automating both RM construction and proposition grounding, potentially broadening the applicability of RMs to more complex, vision-based tasks. It integrates LLMs and VLMs in a novel way for reinforcement learning, which could impact how non-Markovian tasks are specified and learned. RM-VLM is evaluated on three domains: non-Markovian grid-world navigation, robotic manipulation, and 3D object collection, and consistently achieves higher task success rates than existing VLM-based reward methods. The approach uses a VLM-based value function estimator to assess progress towards each RM transition and derive dense rewards.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Reward machines (RMs) are automata-based representations of reward functions in reinforcement learning that capture non-Markovian tasks where credit assignment depends on history. They require a labelling function—a proposition-grounding oracle that maps observations to abstract propositions—which traditionally must be manually engineered for each environment. Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI models that can process and relate visual and textual information, and have been used to generate reward signals from images and task descriptions.

Contrary to the initial hypothesis, domain-specific models do not consistently outperform general-purpose models on specialized tasks without graph grounding.

## [Study Warns Criteria Exposure Lets Embodied Agents Game Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08318) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.08318) investigates Criteria Exposure (CE), the practice of showing a benchmark's success criteria to self-improving embodied coding agents. Through controlled experiments, the authors find that agents with CE quickly achieve higher success rates under the given criteria, but this advantage vanishes when the criteria are slightly altered without changing the underlying instructions, and the learned skills transfer poorly across tasks. This work challenges a common evaluation practice in embodied agent research, suggesting that high benchmark scores may reflect verifier overfitting rather than genuine task learning. It has direct implications for how robot-learning benchmarks are designed and how self-improving agents are compared, affecting researchers and practitioners who rely on these scores to gauge progress. The authors propose instruction-faithful criteria that better reflect the performance of agent-generated programs, and they show that criteria originally written to verify imitation-learning policies often demand more than the corresponding language instructions. The controlled comparison is between a self-improving agent with and without CE, revealing that CE-driven gains are brittle to minor criteria changes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Self-improving embodied agents are increasingly developed and compared on robot simulation benchmarks, where an agent writes robot-control code and is scored by a verifier. Criteria Exposure means giving the agent access to the benchmark's success criteria during learning. In reinforcement learning, a related phenomenon is reward hacking, where a policy optimizes the measurable reward without achieving the intended goal; this paper examines an analogous risk in benchmark-based evaluation of coding agents.

## [Locality-aware quantization improves global structure in compressed AI agent memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08341) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces locality-aware quantization (LAQ), a method that optimizes codebooks in an OPQ index using a hinge objective derived from a proven bound on global fidelity, and shows it raises global fidelity in all 52 held-out runs, including the external FinDER memory, and achieves the highest global fidelity among seven equal-size quantizers in all 12 Multi-Doc-2025 runs. This work addresses a critical gap in AI agent memory compression by showing that local fidelity alone is insufficient and that preserving global structure is essential for tasks like consolidation, deletion, and comparison, which could lead to more reliable and efficient agent memory systems. The paper proves that across the global fidelity band, 1-ν_k is at most 2 to 2.18 times the square root of the near-far rank-violation rate, and that this rate correlates with distance noise (correlation 0.979); LAQ keeps the bytes, rotation, and search of the OPQ index unchanged, and reconstruction alone raises global fidelity in 0 of 52 runs while the far hinge raises it in all 52.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: AI agents store text as embedding vectors and compress them at scale using techniques like vector quantization, which reduces memory footprint but may distort relationships. Local fidelity measures whether nearest neighbors are preserved, while global fidelity measures whether items that should be far apart remain far apart. OPQ (Optimized Product Quantization) is a vector quantization method that rotates and splits vectors into subvectors for efficient compression and search.

## [NPCBench: A New Benchmark for Longitudinal Multimodal Care-Pathway Reasoning in Nasopharyngeal Carcinoma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08369) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced NPCBench, a clinical apprenticeship benchmark for nasopharyngeal carcinoma (NPC) that contains 4,777 component and consultation items and a 100-patient CarePath cohort with 417 scored diagnostic and care tasks. Unlike prior medical benchmarks that score isolated questions, NPCBench evaluates whether models can integrate subspecialty knowledge with multimodal evidence and track a patient's condition across an entire care pathway. The benchmark reveals a composition gap: across 30 evaluated models, strong performance on individual tasks does not guarantee jointly correct diagnostic and care answers for the same patient, with strict CarePath success reaching at most 23% among 21 multimodal models. This shifts medical AI evaluation from static question-answering toward apprenticeship-oriented assessment of clinical workflows, which could better reflect real-world oncology decision-making. NPCBench links stepwise tasks to patient-level care trajectories and uses episode-level evaluation to test whether diagnostic and care answers are jointly correct within a care pathway. Paired controls and physician calibration further examine evidence grounding, longitudinal revision, and consultation judging, though the benchmark is domain-specific to NPC rather than a general medical AI evaluation.

Most existing medical AI benchmarks test isolated factual questions, single-image tasks, or static case vignettes, which do not capture whether a model can track a patient's condition over time. ## [Learned patient world model simulates ICU operations across scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08392) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.08392) proposes learning a reusable patient world model from longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) using a neural network, trained on the MIMIC-IV database, to simulate patient trajectories and care demands across multiple ICU operational scenarios. The frozen model, coupled with scenario-specific mappings to ICU calendars, resources, and queues, reproduces admission demand, care tasks, and discharge proposals for evaluating bed capacity, care dispatch, and post-ICU transfer policies. This approach addresses a key limitation of conventional simulation models that tailor compact state transitions to a single policy question and omit history-dependent dynamics, potentially allowing new care policies to be evaluated by reconfiguring the scenario without rebuilding the model. It could significantly streamline health system simulation for ICU operations and healthcare AI, affecting hospital administrators, policymakers, and researchers. Experiments show the model matches Markov and semi-Markov comparators in aggregate occupancy and directional responses to capacity and dispatch policies, and reproduces policy gains from replayed observed trajectories. Notably, for patients entering the same support state from different prior trajectories, the learned representation captures a 24-hour recovery difference (-6.4 predicted vs -6.7 observed percentage points), a contrast missed by compact state models, which propagates into distinct simulated care demand, queue burden, and bed use.
