## [Latent ODE model reconstructs full-cycle cardiac motion to predict heart failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26718) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers developed a latent dynamical model that encodes bi-ventricular anatomy and full-cycle cine motion as a continuous latent trajectory using heart-rate-aware neural ODE dynamics and a graph-based mesh autoencoder. In 72,386 UK Biobank participants without baseline cardiovascular disease, adding a latent score derived from deviations from a covariate-conditioned prior to refitted pooled cohort equations improved the stratified C-index from 0.704 to 0.785 for predicting incident heart failure. This approach demonstrates that continuous full-cycle modeling of ventricular motion can extract informative cardiac phenotypes beyond conventional cine MRI summaries, potentially improving early heart failure risk prediction. It bridges advanced machine learning with large-scale population health, though external validation is needed before clinical use. The model uses a graph-based mesh autoencoder to reconstruct anatomically consistent 3D+t ventricular motion and a Cox proportional hazards model to test whether deviations from the expected end-diastolic latent state predict incident heart failure. The study included 367 incident heart failure events, and the proposed model outperformed non-graph and non-ODE approaches in reconstruction fidelity, generative realism, and prognostic performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Neural ordinary differential equations (ODEs) are a class of models that define the evolution of a system's state using an ODE governed by a neural network, enabling continuous-time modeling. Graph-based mesh autoencoders extend convolution operations to irregular mesh graphs, useful for representing anatomical shapes like the heart. The Cox proportional hazards model is a survival model that relates the time until an event to covariates, commonly used in medical risk prediction.

## [openJiuwen: A Composable and Adaptive Harness for Long-Horizon Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27969) ⭐️ 7.0/10

openJiuwen introduces an open-source agent harness that combines a shared execution substrate with Rail-based capability composition, enabling developers to build single-agent, delegated sub-agent, and Swarm Flow systems under common execution semantics. It also adapts runtime decisions around a fixed model policy, and achieves 82.6% on SWE-bench Verified and 87.19% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, exceeding the strongest official-leaderboard point estimates by 3.4 and 3.39 percentage points. This work directly addresses two pain points in building long-horizon coding agents—structural composability and runtime adaptivity—which currently force teams to repeatedly rebuild orchestration logic as agent systems grow more complex. If the reported gains hold up, the harness could give researchers and practitioners a reusable foundation for multi-agent coding systems rather than one-off scaffolding. The harness separates a fixed model policy from framework-controlled runtime decisions, letting evolving evidence such as semantic diagnostics, execution outcomes, and task progress dynamically affect context, feedback, and task control. The headline numbers are point estimates from a preprint compared against selected official-leaderboard results, and the work has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: An agent harness, also called agent scaffolding, is the software infrastructure surrounding a large language model that lets it act as an agent—managing tool calls, context, feedback loops, and execution state. Long-horizon coding agents work over evolving repository states across many steps, and increasingly rely on heterogeneous capabilities, delegated sub-agents, and multi-agent coordination. SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1 are benchmarks that evaluate coding agents on real software-engineering tasks and terminal-based workflows, respectively.

## [VOLM Framework Measures Human Value-Add Over LLM in Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00700) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.00700v2) proposes Value Over Language Model (VOLM), a training-free framework that detects original human contribution by reconstructing documents from progressively coarser content representations and comparing them to LLM reconstructions from the task description alone. Evaluated on news articles, ICLR peer reviews, and argumentative essays, VOLM separates human-authored from LLM-generated documents while remaining invariant to content-preserving transformations such as LLM-based reconstruction and round-trip translation. Existing LLM-text detectors largely measure surface-level authorship and are vulnerable to stylistic confounders, so VOLM's focus on informational content addresses a real gap in assessing human contribution in LLM-assisted writing. This has implications for academic integrity, authorship attribution, and fair evaluation of AI-assisted work. The method requires no training or labeled data and never scores the document's surface text, instead extracting content at increasing levels of granularity and comparing LLM reconstructions from partial representations against those from the task description alone. The authors find that increasingly constrained content extractors reduce residual differences between LLM-generated and humanized text, highlighting the need to disentangle informational content from stylistic variation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-generated text detection is typically framed as a binary classification task, with methods falling into black-box detection (using API-level access to analyze outputs) and white-box detection, as well as watermarking, statistics-based detectors, and neural-based detectors. Authorship attribution in NLP instead analyzes stylometric and linguistic features such as word choice, syntactic patterns, and writing style to identify authors. VOLM departs from both by measuring how much information content a person supplied beyond what an LLM could produce from the task description alone.

## [LIFT Transfers LLM Reasoning to VLMs via Vector Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31140) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes LIFT (Language-side reasonIng Facilitation and Transfer), a lightweight vector-intervention method that transfers reasoning capability from a base LLM to an aligned vision-language model without retraining the backbone. LIFT defines Reasoning Vectors as answer-token hidden-state differences between a Reasoner path with an explicit reasoning trace and a Solver path without it, and injects these vectors into the language-side activations of the target VLM. Multimodal scaling often degrades the language-side reasoning ability originally encoded in the base LLM, and this work shows that the base LLM is a more effective source for recovering that ability than the aligned VLM itself. The approach offers a lightweight, training-free path to improve reasoning in VLMs, which could benefit researchers and practitioners building multimodal systems without expensive retraining. LIFT is evaluated on two VLMs across six reasoning benchmarks, and results show that LLM-derived Reasoning Vectors consistently outperform VLM-derived vectors under matched protocols. The method also supports learnable vector adaptation while keeping the VLM backbone frozen, and analyses indicate that the vectors influence intermediate reasoning behavior rather than merely altering final answers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are typically built by extending pretrained Large Language Models (LLMs) with visual modules and multimodal alignment, which adapts the model to process images alongside text. Activation steering or vector intervention is a family of inference-time techniques that modify a model's hidden activations along directions associated with desired behaviors, without changing model weights. This paper applies such intervention to recover reasoning lost during multimodal alignment.

## [Decision-Theoretic Framework Diagnoses and Improves LLM Probabilistic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38005) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes a decision-theoretic framework that decomposes LLM decision loss into belief formation and belief-to-action translation, and evaluates reinforcement learning interventions targeting each component across frontier and open-source models in three domains. This decomposition offers a principled way to diagnose whether LLM failures stem from inaccurate beliefs or poor action selection, which could guide more targeted training and evaluation for decision-assistant applications. Using a synthetic benchmark with known ground truth, the study finds that targeting one component redistributes decision loss without necessarily transferring to others, and that jointly targeting belief formation and decision-making improves both but depends on matched formats between training and evaluation.

## [QES: Contain LLM Backdoors by Quarantining Them in a Disableable Expert](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00663) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Quarantined Expert Shutdown (QES), a containment strategy that allows backdoor formation during training but routes trigger-conditioned behavior into a designated, quarantined expert within a mixture-of-experts-like architecture. At deployment, mitigation is a single constant-time operation: zeroing the quarantined expert's routing weight, which reduces attack success rate from 100% to 0-10% on most settings across two tasks, three attacks, and four model families. This introduces a third paradigm for backdoor defense—"learn, but channel"—beyond suppressing backdoor learning or learning-then-purifying, offering a practical containment approach that avoids trigger screening or weight updates at deployment. It could significantly improve the safety of deploying LLMs trained on potentially poisoned data, affecting AI security practitioners and model deployers. QES augments a Transformer-based language model with routed expert-specific LoRA branches and lightweight routers, using auxiliary routing objectives to attract trigger-conditioned behavior into a designated expert while preserving benign capability elsewhere. The method is computationally efficient and often preserves downstream utility or only modestly affects it, but it assumes a MoE-like architecture and may not directly apply to dense models without such modifications.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Backdoor attacks in LLMs involve injecting a hidden trigger into the model during training so that it behaves normally on benign inputs but produces attacker-specified outputs when the trigger appears. Existing defenses either try to prevent backdoor learning (e.g., filtering poisoned data) or purify the model after training (e.g., repairing weights). Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is an architecture that splits the network into specialized sub-networks (experts) and uses a router to activate only relevant ones per token, enabling scale with minimal compute; QES leverages this routing mechanism to isolate backdoor behavior.

## [Null-Space Projection Purifies LoRA-Tuned LLMs from Backdoors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00685) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.00685v2) proposes a null-space projection method that purifies LoRA-tuned LLMs from backdoor attacks without requiring trigger knowledge, clean reference models, or retraining of the suspect parameters. The method extracts high-fidelity backdoor directions and projects LoRA updates onto orthogonal null spaces per layer or head, reducing attack success rate from nearly 100% to under 10%. This addresses a practical security gap in the increasingly popular LoRA fine-tuning ecosystem, where third-party adapters can silently implant backdoors. By preserving both the base model's general capabilities and the adapter's downstream skills, the approach makes purification deployable without sacrificing utility. The approach is validated progressively from a single layer in text classification to full-parameter LLM generative tasks, using careful data curation and feature approximation to identify backdoor directions. It constructs orthogonal null spaces in both input and output channels and projects LoRA updates onto them, avoiding any post-hoc retraining.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes the base model and trains small low-rank adapter matrices, making it cheap to customize LLMs. However, backdoor attacks can poison fine-tuning data so that a specific trigger causes malicious outputs, and existing defenses often assume knowledge of the trigger or require costly retraining. Null-space projection is a mathematical operation that removes components of a vector lying in a chosen subspace, and here it is used to strip out the backdoor-related directions from LoRA updates.

## [MOF-VERIFY: A Failure-Aware Agentic Harness for MOF Hypothesis Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03056) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a diagnostic benchmark with four task families for LLM-driven hypothesis verification in metal-organic frameworks (MOFs), covering structural grounding, synthesis-condition verification, evidence-sufficiency verification, and MLIP-based computational verification. Guided by diagnosed failure modes, the authors develop MOF-Verify, a failure-aware agentic harness that targets structural, literature, evidence-sufficiency, and computational bottlenecks before producing a final verdict, and report average improvements over direct inference and retrieval-based baselines across multiple backbone LLMs. As LLMs are increasingly embedded as reasoning components in AI-driven materials co-scientists, the reliability of the resulting verification pipeline remains unclear, and this work provides a systematic way to localize where such pipelines fail. The diagnostic benchmark and failure-aware harness could improve trust in AI-assisted scientific discovery for MOFs and offer a template for evaluating agentic verification in other materials domains. Tasks T-MOF-1-3 are evaluated under closed-book, retrieval-enabled, and oracle-evidence settings to separate failures in knowledge access, evidence acquisition, and reasoning, while T-MOF-4 separately evaluates computational verification. Reported gains are concentrated in structural grounding and evidence sufficiency, and benchmark records and release-status documentation are available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) are crystalline porous materials with high surface area and tunable pore structures, widely studied for gas storage, separation, and water treatment. Because MOF structures can appear under different identifiers, synthesis outcomes depend strongly on experimental conditions, and evidence is scattered across heterogeneous sources, verifying hypotheses about them is challenging. Machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) are data-driven surrogate models that reproduce quantum-chemical potential energy surfaces at much lower computational cost, enabling computational verification. An agentic harness is the software layer around an LLM that turns it from a text generator into a working agent by orchestrating tools, retrieval, and validation steps.

<a id="item-196"></a>
## [Trust Layer verifies agent benchmark scores, finds many unearned passes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07274) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a Trust Layer for Agent Evaluation, a post-hoc framework that checks whether recorded benchmark scores are supported by grading logic, earned through traceable computation, honestly reported, and stable across reruns. Applied to five agent configurations on 108 tasks from Agents' Last Exam, only 22.6% of recorded passes cleared all four checks (95% CI 15.0–32.6, n=84), with every model showing untraceable passing runs and 18–46% of tasks failing to stay in one score band over five runs. This work highlights that measuring what an agent can do and verifying that it actually did it are distinct problems, and current benchmarks address only the former. The findings suggest that reported agent benchmark scores may overstate true capability, which could mislead researchers, practitioners, and users relying on leaderboards for deployment decisions. The first three checks rely only on saved artifacts, while the fourth requires re-running the agent; model judgments are used only to label evidence under majority voting, and all verdicts follow deterministic rules without modifying the recorded score. The framework is additive and post-hoc, meaning it can be applied to existing benchmark results without changing the original evaluation process.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Agents' Last Exam is a benchmark designed to evaluate AI agents on long-horizon, economically valuable professional workflows with verifiable outcomes, often in full OS environments. As LLM-based agents become more capable, benchmarks have become the primary way to compare them, but most benchmarks report a single deterministic score without checking whether the agent's success was legitimate or reproducible. This paper addresses that gap by proposing a trust layer that audits benchmark scores after the fact.

## [MedZERO: Self-Evolving Agents for Open-Ended Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08327) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MedZERO introduces a self-evolving framework that pairs an Examiner, which generates frontier medical question-option pairs, with a Reasoner that solves them via evidence-grounded multi-turn reasoning using external knowledge tools. It achieves the highest average accuracy on five public medical reasoning benchmarks at both 4B and 8B model scales under open-ended evaluation. Most self-evolving methods target easily verifiable domains like math and coding, leaving open-ended, knowledge-intensive medical reasoning underexplored. MedZERO addresses this gap by enabling continual improvement without costly expert supervision, which could make medical AI more scalable and adaptable. MedZERO adopts controlled knowledge accumulation, maintaining temporary exploratory knowledge alongside curated persistent knowledge during reasoning. The framework is evaluated on five public medical reasoning benchmarks using 4B- and 8B-scale base models, with code available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Self-evolving agents are LLM-based systems that improve through iterative task generation and problem-solving, typically relying on verifiable feedback such as exact answers or executable programs. Medical reasoning is open-ended, knowledge-intensive, and often only partially verifiable, making such feedback hard to obtain. MedZERO tackles this by combining an Examiner that creates challenging medical questions with a Reasoner that uses external knowledge tools to ground its answers.

## [SCOPE and ClosureBench shift symbolic regression to conjecture-driven discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18938) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SCOPE, a multi-agent framework in which multiple scientist agents independently pursue scientific conjectures through equation modeling, question formulation, and active experimentation, then exchange evidence to revise their conjectures. They also released ClosureBench, an interactive benchmark of 60 closure-discovery tasks across five scientific domains, where agents control OpenFOAM experiments to acquire data for equation discovery. This work reframes symbolic regression from merely fitting equations to evaluating whether the underlying scientific conjecture should be retained, revised, or rejected, which could make AI-driven discovery more scientifically meaningful. ClosureBench's new metrics—evidence faithfulness, mechanism insight, and experimental effectiveness—provide a way to assess the discovery process rather than just equation accuracy, potentially influencing how AI for science systems are evaluated. ClosureBench embeds an unknown algebraic relation in a known PDE system, and agents must design experiments in OpenFOAM to collect new data, with evaluation going beyond equation accuracy to include evidence faithfulness, mechanism insight, and experimental effectiveness. SCOPE reportedly achieves higher equation accuracy than diverse baselines on both ClosureBench and a real-world scientific discovery case, while producing more accurate, reliable, and effective scientific discoveries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Symbolic regression is a machine learning technique that searches the space of mathematical expressions to find a model that best fits a dataset while balancing accuracy and simplicity, and it has been used to rediscover physical laws from data. Traditional symbolic regression methods optimize candidate equations directly, but improving an equation's fit does not establish whether its underlying scientific conjecture is valid, since a conjecture may admit multiple mathematical realizations. Multi-agent systems for AI for science, such as SciAgents and Robin, have recently emerged to automate hypothesis generation and experimentation, and SCOPE extends this paradigm to conjecture-driven equation discovery.

A new arXiv paper (2610.22915v2) introduces Reward Engine, which constructs a frozen RewardPack of testcases for correctness and rubrics for graded quality from a task and its initial trajectories. The RewardPack then guides the agent to revise and select outputs and can serve as a reward for reinforcement learning. Open-ended tool agents rarely have executable ground-truth rewards, so this work offers a way to derive a stationary reward from existing experience that exposes current policy failures. It could improve consistency and reliability of LLM agents in office and coding tasks, impacting agent developers and RL researchers. On WorkBuddy Bench Office and Claw-Eval, the constructed reward agrees with benchmark graders on 82.3% and 80.5% of candidate pairs without reversing their ordering, and used for revision it raises three-run consistency (Pass^3) from 45.0% to 54.0% and from 78.4% to 83.7%. The paper is an arXiv preprint with results only partially shown and no community discussion yet.

## [PhysTrace: Training-Free MLLM Evaluation of Physical Consistency in Video Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31429) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PhysTrace introduces a training-free framework that uses multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to formulate history-conditioned physical expectations and track their agreement with observed video windows via temporal video-language alignment. On the IntPhys2 benchmark, it achieves 67.2% possible-impossible discrimination accuracy, outperforming direct MLLM baselines, and reaches up to 77.4% Hit Rate on the new IntPhys2-Timing benchmark for temporal localization of violations. This work addresses a key gap in evaluating video generative models for world action models, where physical consistency is history-dependent and cannot be captured by simple plausibility judgments. It provides a scalable, training-free evaluation method that could become a standard for assessing whether video generators truly understand physical dynamics, impacting research in prediction, planning, and action generation. The framework also introduces reference-guided masked denoising for controlled reconstruction from mixed possible-impossible video latents, and the IntPhys2-Timing benchmark adds violation intervals to IntPhys2. Human evaluations on these reconstructions reveal model-dependent differences in physical consistency and fewer physically impossible outcomes at higher initial noise levels, trends broadly captured by PhysTrace.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Video generative models based on diffusion or flow matching are increasingly used as world models for prediction and planning, but evaluating their physical understanding is challenging. IntPhys2 is a benchmark that tests intuitive physics understanding in AI systems using videos with realistic occlusions. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can process both video and text, enabling alignment between visual content and language descriptions. PhysTrace leverages these capabilities to assess whether generated videos adhere to physical constraints over time.

## [MORE: Self-Evolving Agents Adapt Reasoning Mode and Effort](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33684) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose MORE, a self-evolving training framework that adaptively selects both reasoning form (direct answering, short or long chain-of-thought, or code-based reasoning) and computational effort for each task. It uses a two-stage paradigm: Stage 1 builds a compact prior over reasoning modes, and Stage 2 self-evolves this prior with Mode-Adaptive and Test-time Effort PPO (MATE-PPO) on generated, verifiable task streams without curated data. MORE addresses a real inefficiency in self-evolving agent training, where costly deliberation is applied uniformly across all tasks. By learning to allocate concise reasoning to easy tasks and extended deliberation only where useful, it could improve both accuracy and inference efficiency for LLM agents across coding, math, and general reasoning. Across coding, mathematics, and general-reasoning benchmarks, MORE achieves consistent performance gains on Base and Coder backbones at model scales from 3B to 14B. During inference, it demonstrates higher accuracy and better efficiency, with markedly shorter response lengths and reduced inference time.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting encourages LLMs to generate intermediate reasoning steps before answering, improving accuracy on complex tasks. Self-evolving training uses verifiable feedback to improve agents on difficult tasks, but typically applies the same costly deliberation to every task. PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization) is a popular reinforcement learning algorithm used for post-training LLMs, and MORE extends it with mode-adaptive and test-time effort control.

## [AutoVend: A 180-Day Benchmark for LLM Agents in Vending Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36980) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AutoVend introduces a 180-day simulated benchmark in which a single LLM agent manages 20 vending machines across five site types via a Web-based operating interface, using a data-grounded simulator with a shared warehouse, procurement lead times, paid replenishment, inventory expiration, and recurring operating costs. The study evaluates five LLM agents against a rule-based inventory-control baseline, finding that four of five fall short of the baseline and that returns per dollar of inference cost rank models differently from returns alone. This benchmark shifts agent evaluation from single transactions to sustained economic returns in online-to-offline commerce, an underexplored area where online decisions incur costly physical execution. It highlights that current LLM agents struggle with long-horizon operational planning and that cost-efficiency can reorder model rankings, which matters for anyone deploying agents in real-world business operations. The simulator models a shared warehouse, procurement lead times, paid replenishment, inventory expiration, and recurring operating costs, and the analysis diagnoses site coordination, execution, and performance over time. Trajectory analysis reveals difficulties in grounding feasible actions, managing inventory commitments, and converting feedback into effective policy revisions, and gains in individual metrics do not consistently translate into stronger economic growth because inventory and execution costs erode them.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used to automate multi-step tasks, but long-horizon planning—sustaining coherent decisions over extended periods—remains a known weakness. Online-to-offline (O2O) commerce blends digital ordering with physical fulfillment, so evaluating agents there requires measuring whether online decisions produce durable economic returns rather than just successful individual transactions. Rule-based inventory control, which uses fixed reorder policies, provides a standard baseline for comparison.

## [GAAC: Lossless KV Cache Compression for Efficient LLM Inference on Personal Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38210) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GAAC introduces a lossless BF16 KV cache compression and serving system that encodes exponents using calibrated per-channel base-plus-offset codes, achieving 1.405–1.407× compression across five models. A fused attention kernel reconstructs KV tiles on the fly, accelerating decode attention by 1.19–1.27× at 64K context on NVIDIA DGX Spark and by 1.02–1.25× on Apple M4. Long-context LLM inference on personal devices is increasingly bottlenecked by KV cache memory, and GAAC's lossless approach avoids quality degradation while reducing resident KV memory by ~26% and boosting throughput by 13–29%. This makes it a practical step toward deploying long-context models on edge hardware without approximation trade-offs. The compression ratio is modest (~1.4×) and performance varies with precision: FP8 E4M3 caches compress only 1.09–1.14× and E5M2 caches 1.25–1.30×. The system supports block-wise compression and fixed-capacity slots for incremental updates and paged serving, with code available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: The KV cache stores key and value tensors from previous tokens to avoid recomputation during autoregressive generation, but its size grows linearly with context length and can dominate memory on personal devices. BF16 is a 16-bit floating-point format with an 8-bit exponent (same range as FP32) and 7-bit mantissa, and its exponent field exhibits redundancy that GAAC exploits. Fused attention kernels, like FlashAttention, combine attention operations into a single GPU kernel to reduce memory traffic, and GAAC extends this idea by reconstructing compressed KV tiles on the fly.

AuroraWorld proposes a structured 4D world modeling approach to synthesize physically consistent egocentric robot manipulation videos from exocentric footage, addressing viewpoint transformation and occlusion challenges. The authors state that code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/xueha0/AuroraWorld. Egocentric data is crucial for visuomotor policy learning but expensive to collect at scale, so a method that reliably converts abundant exocentric videos into policy-relevant egocentric views could significantly lower the cost of training embodied AI systems. This work targets a real bottleneck in robot learning and is relevant to the broader robotics and embodied AI community. The method must handle substantial viewpoint transformations, severe occlusions, complex object interactions, and inherently sparse observations from available exocentric viewpoints, which are the specific failure modes of existing synthesis approaches. As an arXiv abstract, detailed quantitative results and limitations are not yet available, though a public code release is promised.

## [ORSD: Dynamic Process Rubrics for Token-Level Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06543) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Online Rubric Self-Distillation (ORSD), a method that generates natural-language process rubrics from on-policy trajectories and uses them as privileged information to compute token-level credit, which is added to the trajectory-level GRPO advantage via additive advantage shaping. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that ORSD accelerates policy improvement and boosts final task performance for Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-4B under the same interaction budget. This work addresses the credit assignment bottleneck in reinforcement learning for LLM agents by bridging sparse outcome rewards and dense process supervision, potentially enabling more sample-efficient and effective training of agentic LLMs. It offers a self-contained approach that does not require external reward models or human annotations, which could be widely adopted in the agent RL community. ORSD uses a frozen verifier to select one rubric bundle per step based on bundle-level alignment between rubric satisfaction and task success, then compares token log-likelihoods with and without the rubric to derive token-specific process credit. The method is evaluated only on ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen3 models, so generalization to other domains and larger models remains to be tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Credit assignment is a fundamental challenge in reinforcement learning, especially for long-horizon tasks where a single outcome reward must be distributed across many decisions. In LLM agent training, methods like GRPO rely on trajectory-level rewards, which provide sparse signals and struggle to identify which specific tokens or actions were critical. Process supervision offers denser feedback but typically requires external reward models or step-level annotations, which are costly to obtain.

## [Spectral Neural Networks Extend Implicit Regularization to Nonlinear Matrix Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.17595) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Spectral Neural Networks (SNN), a matrix network architecture where each neuron applies a nonlinear activation to the singular values of the input matrix. It proves that under gradient flow, the squared loss decays exponentially fast during training, and that the training implicitly selects an approximate solution minimizing the nuclear norm. This work extends the phenomenon of implicit regularization—where gradient descent converges to regularized solutions without explicit regularizers—to nonlinear spectral parameterizations, offering a new perspective on generalization in neural networks. It could impact the design of matrix learning algorithms and theoretical understanding of deep learning optimization. The analysis is conducted under gradient flow, a continuous approximation of discrete gradient descent, and the empirical investigations demonstrate the effectiveness of SNN in matrix sensing tasks. The architecture coordinatizes matrices by their singular values and singular vectors rather than by their entries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Implicit regularization refers to the tendency of gradient descent to find solutions that generalize well even without explicit regularization, a key factor in neural networks' success. Matrix sensing is a fundamental problem in signal processing and machine learning, where the goal is to reconstruct a low-rank matrix from limited linear measurements. Singular value decomposition (SVD) factorizes a matrix into singular values and singular vectors, and is widely used for low-rank approximation.

## [Robust Q-Learning Algorithm for Finite Model Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.04259) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes a robust Q-learning algorithm for Markov decision processes where each state-action pair has a finite ambiguity set of candidate transition kernels. It establishes almost sure convergence to the robust optimum and derives a time-uniform high-probability error certificate that separates observable empirical Bellman residual from transition-kernel estimation error. This provides theoretical guarantees for robust reinforcement learning under finite model uncertainty, which is important for safety-critical applications where transition probabilities are uncertain. The error certificate allows practitioners to quantify uncertainty and make informed decisions. The algorithm only requires observing successor states and deterministic rewards from each candidate simulator, without explicit knowledge of transition probabilities or reward functions. The authors also provide quantitative finite-ambiguity approximation results for Wasserstein and parametric uncertainty models on finite state spaces, and compare the tabular algorithm with robust value iteration numerically.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Q-learning is a model-free reinforcement learning algorithm that learns the value of state-action pairs without requiring a model of the environment. Robust reinforcement learning addresses uncertainty in the environment's dynamics by considering a set of possible models (ambiguity set) and optimizing for the worst-case performance. Finite ambiguity sets mean the uncertainty is represented by a finite number of candidate transition kernels, which can be user-designed to include competing fitted models or stress scenarios.

## [Teacher-student ML reveals declining groundwater across Bangladesh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22771) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers developed a teacher-student machine learning framework that reconstructs high-resolution (2-km) groundwater depth maps for Bangladesh from sparse in-situ measurements, achieving R² = 0.93 over 2003-2022 and generalizing to unseen years and districts. The reconstruction reveals widespread water-table deepening and a national groundwater recharge decline of 1.10 mm/yr (p < 0.001), with significant recharge declines in 55.6% of the country versus increases in only 4.9%. This work provides a scalable method for monitoring groundwater in data-sparse aquifers globally, addressing a critical gap in observational capabilities. The findings challenge the Bengal Water Machine hypothesis by showing that pumping-induced recharge enhancement remains localized rather than widespread, with significant implications for water resource management in Bangladesh and similar regions. The teacher model learns from sparse annual in-situ minimum (R² = 0.86) and maximum (R² = 0.96) depth observations to generate dense spatial patterns, while the student model transfers these patterns from 25-km GLDAS groundwater storage to 2-km depth maps using location-specific features. The student model generalizes across unseen years (Leave-One-Year-Out R² = 0.93) and locations (Leave-One-District-Out R² = 0.70), enabling reconstruction without contemporary in-situ measurements.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Groundwater is a vital water source in Bangladesh, but sparse in-situ measurements limit understanding of its dynamics. Machine learning can help fill gaps, but existing approaches struggle to generalize across time and space. The teacher-student framework is a machine learning paradigm where a teacher model transfers knowledge to a student model to improve learning efficiency and generalization. GLDAS (Global Land Data Assimilation System) provides coarse-resolution groundwater storage data that can be downscaled using such frameworks.

## [Poisoned Distillation Data Can Backdoor Student Models Despite Clean Teacher](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21323) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2504.21323v5) demonstrates that knowledge distillation can be backdoored by poisoning the distillation dataset with triggered images that a clean teacher already predicts as an attacker-chosen target label. The attack remains effective even at a 10% poisoning rate and works across multiple manipulation strategies, including targeted adversarial perturbations and targeted GAN-based class transitions. This challenges the common assumption that knowledge distillation is safe when the teacher model is clean, showing that the integrity of distillation data is part of the security boundary. It has significant implications for any pipeline that uses distillation for model compression, as attackers can compromise student models without touching the teacher. The attack injects triggered images that the clean teacher already classifies as the target label, so the student learns a backdoor while the teacher remains unaffected. The paper evaluates targeted adversarial perturbations and targeted GAN-based class transitions, and shows that the backdoored student maintains competitive accuracy on clean images.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Knowledge distillation trains a smaller student model to mimic a larger teacher model's outputs, and is widely used for model compression. Backdoor attacks typically inject poisoned data with triggers during supervised training so the model misbehaves when the trigger appears. Prior work assumed distillation was immune to such attacks if the teacher was clean, but this paper shows that poisoning the distillation dataset itself can bypass that assumption.

## [CoMPAS3D: Salsa Dataset and Benchmark for Interactive Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19684) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced CoMPAS3D, a motion capture dataset of improvised partner salsa dancing with over 2,800 expert-annotated segments from 18 dancers across three skill levels, along with a benchmark for evaluating interactive motion generation. The benchmark defines three tasks—move classification, proficiency estimation, and follower generation—and proposes two objective metrics (move legibility and proficiency appropriateness) that go beyond kinematic measures like FID and beat alignment. This work addresses a key limitation in interactive motion generation: existing metrics cannot assess whether generated movements are semantically appropriate within a shared movement vocabulary. By providing a structured evaluation domain inspired by games like Go and Chess, CoMPAS3D could significantly benefit human-robot interaction and motion generation research, enabling more socially aware humanoid robots. The dataset comprises 3 hours of improvisation by 18 dancers spanning beginner, intermediate, and professional levels, with fine-grained annotations of move types, execution errors, and stylistic elements. Fine-tuned vision-language models perform well on ground-truth sequences and correlate with expert judgments, but when applied to generated dances from Duolando and InterGen, the metrics reveal a large gap between ground-truth and generated motions, corroborated by human evaluations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Interactive motion generation involves creating models that can produce human-like movements in response to a partner, crucial for socially interactive humanoids. Traditional evaluation relies on kinematic metrics like FID (Fréchet Inception Distance) and beat alignment, which measure statistical similarity and timing but not semantic appropriateness. Salsa dancing serves as an ideal testbed because it is dyadic, improvisational, and governed by a rich vocabulary of moves and community-validated judging criteria.

## [RLSF: Self-Feedback Reinforcement Learning Post-Training for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21931) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Reinforcement Learning from Self-Feedback (RLSF), a post-training method that uses a frozen LLM's intrinsic confidence to rank multiple Chain-of-Thought decoding beams and create synthetic preference pairs for fine-tuning. This approach improves arithmetic reasoning and multiple-choice QA without human labels, gold answers, or external reward models. RLSF eliminates the need for costly human preference data and external reward models, offering a data-efficient and self-contained alignment pipeline. It could make post-training more accessible and scalable, especially for reasoning-intensive tasks where labeled data is scarce. The method generates multiple CoT decoding beams from a frozen LLM, computes confidence for each final answer span, and ranks traces to form preferences for standard preference optimization. It simultaneously improves calibration and step-by-step reasoning, but relies on the model's intrinsic confidence being meaningful and may inherit biases from the base model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning allows LLMs to solve complex problems by generating intermediate steps, and prior work shows CoT paths can be elicited by altering decoding without prompting. Preference optimization methods like DPO fine-tune LLMs using pairs of preferred and rejected responses, typically requiring human labels or a reward model. RLSF combines these ideas by using the model's own confidence as a self-generated reward signal.

## [LSD-3D generates large-scale 3D driving scenes with geometry grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19204) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LSD-3D proposes a method that directly generates large-scale 3D driving scenes with accurate geometry by combining proxy geometry generation and environment representation with score distillation from 2D image priors. This enables causal novel view synthesis with object permanence and explicit 3D geometry estimation, conditioned on map layouts for high controllability. This work bridges the gap between neural reconstruction methods, which are limited to static captures, and diffusion-based generation, which lacks geometry grounding, providing a controllable way to generate diverse and geometrically consistent driving data for training and testing autonomous systems. The method uses score distillation from learned 2D image priors to guide the generation of proxy geometry and environment representation, allowing prompt-guided geometry and high-fidelity texture and structure. It produces realistic and geometrically consistent 3D generations of complex driving scenes, though the approach may inherit limitations from the 2D priors and proxy representation.

**Background**: Neural reconstruction methods can reconstruct 3D scenes from sensor data but are limited to static environments and lack scene control. Diffusion models can generate diverse images and videos but often lack 3D geometry grounding and causality. Novel view synthesis aims to generate images from new viewpoints, and proxy geometry provides a coarse 3D representation to anchor generation. LSD-3D combines these ideas to generate large-scale 3D driving scenes with explicit geometry.

## [FinMultiHopQA: A KG-Grounded Multi-Hop Financial QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02906) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FinMultiHopQA introduces a benchmark of 15,600 qualitative and quantitative questions grounded in FinReflectKG, a temporally indexed financial knowledge graph derived from SEC 10-K filings with over 17.5 million triples. The pipeline generates statistically salient 2-3 hop typed knowledge graph motifs across intra-document, inter-year, and cross-company regimes, enabling controlled evaluation of evidence-driven reasoning. This benchmark fills a gap in financial question answering by jointly supporting controlled multi-hop taxonomies, document topologies, motif-level reproducibility, and provenance-linked auditability at scale. It enables researchers to study how evidence presentation affects reasoning LLMs' performance, which is crucial for developing more reliable financial NLP systems. Using six different presentations of the same evidence, the study finds that reasoning LLMs' judged correctness is consistently lower under less-structured presentations, most sharply for quantitative cross-company reasoning and 3-hop branch motifs. The benchmark also shows that KG-linked presentation uses fewer input tokens by design, and the authors release the benchmark, provenance annotations, model outputs, and evaluation records.

**Background**: Multi-hop question answering requires composing evidence distributed across multiple documents or sections, and in finance, this often means connecting data from different filing sections, fiscal years, and peer companies. SEC 10-K filings are comprehensive annual reports that publicly traded companies file with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, containing detailed financial performance and risk information. Knowledge graphs represent facts as triples (subject-predicate-object), and typed motifs are recurring patterns of such triples that can be used to generate complex reasoning questions.

## [Instruction-Tuning Open LLMs on Medical Classification Data Boosts German Tumor Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.13624) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new study fine-tuned eight open-weight LLMs (Qwen, Llama, Mistral; 7–70B parameters) on over 500,000 question-answer pairs generated from ICD-10-GM, ICD-O-3, and OPS catalogues, raising ICD-10-GM exact accuracy from 1.4–24% to 41–58% and partial accuracy from 31–74% to 73–83%. The work also established data-quality ceilings of 60–79% exact and 81–94% partial derivation for ICD-10 coding. This demonstrates that public medical classification catalogues can be leveraged to build instruction datasets that substantially improve open-weight LLMs on clinical documentation tasks, offering a privacy-preserving alternative to large proprietary models for German hospitals. It also provides a rigorous ceiling analysis that helps set realistic expectations for automation in medical coding. ICD-O-3 topography coding improved but remained much lower (exact 22–40%, partial 56–67%), malformed code outputs dropped to 0% for all models, and tumor-diagnosis recognition reached 99%; accuracy correlated with model size but gaps narrowed after fine-tuning, and Qwen3's reasoning mode performed worse and was over 100 times slower than fine-tuning.

## [StreamGaze: First Benchmark for Gaze-Guided Temporal Reasoning in Streaming Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01707) ⭐️ 7.0/10

StreamGaze is introduced as the first benchmark to evaluate how Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) utilize human gaze signals for past, present, and proactive temporal reasoning in streaming videos. It includes a gaze-video QA generation pipeline that aligns egocentric videos with raw gaze trajectories through fixation extraction, region-specific visual prompting, and scanpath construction. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in streaming video understanding by incorporating gaze as a cue for anticipating user intent, which is essential for applications like AR glasses. It reveals substantial performance gaps between state-of-the-art MLLMs and humans, highlighting limitations in gaze-based temporal reasoning and proactive prediction. The benchmark includes tasks requiring immediate responses (past and present) and proactive tasks where models must continuously process frames and respond at the appropriate future moment. Detailed analyses of gaze prompting strategies, reasoning behaviors, and task-specific failure modes are provided, and all data and code are publicly available.

Gaze signals convey social attention and user focus, and recent works like GazeCoT have explored gaze estimation for MLLMs, but StreamGaze is the first to systematically evaluate gaze-guided temporal and proactive reasoning.

FacEDiT introduces a self-supervised facial motion infilling framework that allows editing local segments of talking head videos (insertion, deletion, substitution) while preserving identity and continuity. The method uses a mask-controlled model with local temporal attention bias and temporal smoothness regularization, and the authors also release FacEDiTBench, the first benchmark for talking head video editing. This work addresses a challenging and underexplored problem in video editing: modifying a local segment of a talking head video without reshooting, which standard speech-driven generation and lip sync methods cannot handle. The unified framework also extends to portrait animation and lip synchronization, potentially impacting content creation, dubbing, and post-production workflows. The self-supervised pretext task simulates local editing by masking a motion span and reconstructing it from speech and visible motion context, avoiding the need for paired videos. The model uses a local temporal attention bias and temporal smoothness regularization to improve lip-speech alignment and transition continuity, and the same model can perform portrait animation and lip sync by changing the mask pattern.

**Background**: Talking head video editing involves modifying a portion of a video of a person speaking, such as inserting or deleting a word, while keeping the rest unchanged. Self-supervised learning trains models on auxiliary tasks (pretext tasks) using unlabeled data, and here the pretext task is infilling masked facial motion from speech and context.

## [PAVE: Early-Exit DNN Verification Speeds Up 80% of Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20755) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose PAVE (PAreto VErification), a method that augments deep neural networks with early exits and verifies robustness exit-by-exit, solving roughly 80% of queries in a fraction of the time required for direct verification. The approach incurs at most a 0.3% accuracy change and includes a formal proof that the new robustness property is fixed-parameter tractable under a trace stability condition. DNN verification is NP-hard and often times out on modern architectures, so PAVE's ability to solve a large fraction of queries quickly could make formal robustness verification more practical for real-world deployment. The work also bridges early-exit networks and formal methods, potentially enabling verification of larger models like ResNet-18 on CIFAR-100. PAVE introduces two sound optimizations—break (early termination when the winner dominates) and continue (skipping the inner loop when no runner-up can win)—reducing SAFE-case verification time by up to 10x. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 with networks from 600K to 33M parameters show PAVE solves queries that were previously unsolved.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Formal verification of DNNs aims to prove that a network's output remains stable under small input perturbations, a property known as robustness, but the problem is NP-hard and exact tools often time out. Early-exit networks are a class of dynamic neural networks that allow inference to stop at intermediate layers when confident, reducing computation. PAVE combines these ideas by verifying the early-exit behavior, which is often tractable, before analyzing the full network.

EmboTeam introduces a neuro-symbolic framework that uses LLM-generated state expectations, verified via PDDL symbolic search, and grounded into a team-level parallel behavior tree for heterogeneous multi-robot collaboration. The framework uses robot-specific subtrees for precondition checking, skill execution, effect validation, local replanning, and blackboard-based synchronization, enabling reactive grounding. ## [First Instruction-Level Fault Injection Study of LLM Inference on GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19912) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper presents the first instruction-level fault injection study of LLM inference on GPUs, systematically analyzing how soft errors affect large language models. It reveals reliability characteristics across model architecture, parameter scale, and task complexity, showing that LLM vulnerability differs from prior neural networks. As LLMs are increasingly deployed in radiation-prone environments such as satellite computing, understanding their resilience to soft errors is critical for reliable operation. The findings could inform fault tolerance mechanisms and hardware design for LLM deployment in safety-critical scenarios. The study uses fault injection to characterize silent data corruptions (SDCs) and detected uncorrectable errors (DUEs) during inference, and highlights that LLM resilience is influenced by architecture, scale, and task complexity. As an academic study, it provides insights but does not yet offer a complete mitigation solution.

<a id="item-221"></a>
## [WoVR: Reliable World Models for RL Post-Training of VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13977) ⭐️ 7.0/10

WoVR introduces a reliable world-model-based reinforcement learning framework that explicitly regulates interaction with imperfect imagined dynamics to stably post-train Vision-Language-Action policies without massive real-world robot interaction. It achieves this through a controllable action-conditioned video world model, Keyframe-Initialized Rollouts to reduce effective error depth, and World Model-Policy co-evolution to maintain alignment. This work addresses a critical bottleneck in RL post-training of VLA models: world-model hallucination and long-horizon error accumulation that mislead policy optimization. By enabling stable long-horizon imagined rollouts, WoVR shows that world models can serve as practical simulators for RL, potentially reducing reliance on costly real-world robot interaction and accelerating deployment of VLA policies. Keyframe-Initialized Rollouts (KIR) are implemented as a change to the initial context fed into the world model rather than a change to the model architecture, and the world model uses a causal 3D VAE (Wan encoder) whose temporal input length follows a specific form. Experiments show superior LIBERO performance and consistent real-world gains across multiple robotic platforms.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, such as Google DeepMind's RT-2, combine vision and language understanding to directly map instructions and images to robot actions. Reinforcement learning can unlock capabilities beyond imitation learning for these models, but requires massive real-world interaction. Learned world models—neural networks that predict future states given actions—can serve as simulators for model-based RL, but closed-loop imagined rollouts suffer from hallucination and error accumulation, providing unreliable learning signals.

## [RoboCurate verifies synthetic robot data by executing predicted actions in simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18742) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RoboCurate introduces a framework that verifies synthetic robot data by executing actions predicted by an inverse dynamics model (IDM) in a simulator and measuring motion agreement between the simulator rollout and the generated video. It also expands scene diversity using off-the-shelf generative models with structured editing axes and pre-filters physically implausible videos with a VLM before action verification. This addresses a key bottleneck in robot learning: noisy action labels in synthetic data that hinder policy training. The reported relative improvements in success rates—+90.3% on GR-1 Tabletop, +29.1% on DexMimicGen, and +179.9%/+42.9% on real-world humanoid benchmarks—suggest that action-verified synthetic data can significantly boost robot learning performance. The method relies on an IDM to predict actions from generated videos and a simulator to execute them, measuring motion agreement as a verification signal. It also uses a VLM for pre-filtering, but the approach assumes access to a simulator and may inherit inaccuracies from the IDM or video generator.

<a id="item-223"></a>
## [Trainable Frequencies in Quantum Models: Reach, Grids, and Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23409) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper shows that the target-driven part of the prefactor gradient in trainable-frequency quantum models is a weighted sum of slopes of the overlap sin u/u, giving each frequency a reach of half a resolution step. It proposes a ternary prefactor grid that places a model frequency within half a step of every band frequency using logarithmically many encoding gates, and validates it with Adam training on single- and multi-frequency circuits. This work provides a theoretical understanding of how far trainable frequencies can move, which is crucial for designing quantum machine learning models that can fit target functions with arbitrary frequency spectra. It offers practical guidance on initialization and grid design, potentially improving the expressivity and trainability of quantum models. Under gradient flow, an isolated real frequency with optimally adapting coefficient reaches its target from within half a step, but a fixed coefficient can encounter a phase-dependent barrier. The ternary grid improved successful fits on synthetic targets from 69% to 84% compared to the best random start, though with many target frequencies, training the fixed grid longer sometimes fitted better than training the prefactors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Trainable-frequency quantum models are a generalization of quantum models where the frequencies of the Fourier series output are not fixed but can be trained by adding parameters to the encoding gates. Prior work showed that training rarely moves a frequency more than one resolution step away from its initial value. This paper analyzes the gradient to explain this limitation and proposes a grid-based initialization to overcome it.

## [ADV-0: Preference-Guided Adversarial Policy Optimization for Long-Tail Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15221) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces ADV-0, a closed-loop min-max optimization framework that models the interaction between a driving policy (defender) and an adversarial agent (attacker) as a zero-sum Markov game. By aligning the attacker's utility directly with the defender's objective, ADV-0 casts dynamic adversary evolution as iterative preference learning, providing an algorithm-agnostic solution that provably converges to a Nash Equilibrium and maximizes a certified lower bound on real-world performance. This work addresses a key weakness in adversarial training for autonomous driving: the decoupling of scenario generation from policy optimization, which causes objective misalignment and fails to track evolving failure modes. By tightly coupling attacker and defender objectives, ADV-0 could significantly improve the robustness and generalization of driving policies and motion planners against rare but safety-critical long-tail scenarios. ADV-0 provides theoretical guarantees of convergence to a Nash Equilibrium and maximizes a certified lower bound on real-world performance, while being algorithm-agnostic so it can wrap around different policy optimization methods. Experiments show it exposes diverse safety-critical failures and enhances generalizability to unseen long-tail risks for both learned policies and motion planners.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Autonomous driving systems must handle long-tail scenarios—rare but safety-critical events—that are underrepresented in real-world data. Adversarial training is a common approach: an attacker generates challenging scenarios while a defender (the driving policy) learns to handle them. Zero-sum Markov games provide a formal framework for such two-player competitive interactions, and iterative preference learning is a technique that adjusts models over repeated rounds using pairwise preference feedback rather than absolute rewards.

## [Study finds higher explanation correctness does not always improve human understanding in XAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25251) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A controlled user study with 200 participants manipulated explanation correctness at four levels (100%, 85%, 70%, 55%) in a synthetic time series classification task. Results show that forward simulation accuracy dropped only at 70% and 55% compared to 100%, with no conclusive difference between 85% and 100%, indicating that not all differences in functional correctness translate to differences in human understanding. This finding challenges the common assumption in XAI evaluation that higher faithfulness metrics automatically lead to better human understanding, suggesting that functional correctness metrics need to be validated against human outcomes. It has significant implications for researchers and practitioners designing interpretable AI systems, as they may need to reconsider how they evaluate explanation quality. The study used a synthetic time series classification task where participants could not rely on domain knowledge or visual intuition, and explanations were constructed to resemble feature attributions with correctness defined against a known ground truth. Notably, lower correctness reduced the number of participants who predicted accurately rather than uniformly lowering accuracy, and even with fully correct explanations, only a subset of participants predicted well above chance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Explainable AI (XAI) methods are often evaluated using functional correctness metrics, also called faithfulness or fidelity, which measure how closely an explanation reflects the model's actual reasoning. It is commonly assumed that higher correctness leads to better human understanding, but this link had not been tested with controlled levels of correctness. Feature attributions are a popular type of explanation that highlight which input features were important for a model's decision.

A web-based user interface is provided for automated hypothesis testing, though the system's validation is currently limited to simulation rather than real-world experiments.

## [Fixed-point framework explains when looped transformers generalize](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15259) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2604.15259v3) introduces a fixed-point based framework that analyzes looped transformers along three stability axes—local attractivity, input-dependence, and geometry—and proves that looped networks without recall have countable fixed points and cannot achieve strong input-dependence, while recall with outer normalization yields a regime where fixed points are locally attractive, locally smooth, and stably backpropagated. The authors also propose internal recall, a novel recall placement variant with an identity residual path, and empirically validate the framework's predictions on chess, sudoku, and prefix-sums tasks. This work addresses a key open question in test-time compute scaling: which architectural choices allow looped transformers to extrapolate to harder problems rather than memorize training-specific solutions. By linking architectural components like recall and normalization to provable stability and generalization properties, it offers concrete guidance for designing more reliable and efficient recursive transformer architectures. The theoretical results show that looped networks without recall have countable fixed points and cannot achieve strong input-dependence at any spectral regime, whereas recall with outer normalization reliably produces a regime where fixed points are simultaneously locally attractive, locally smooth in the input, and supported by stable backpropagation. The empirical validation uses small looped transformers on chess, sudoku, and prefix-sums, and the novel internal recall variant allows an identity residual path, whose contrasting fixed-point behavior is used to test the framework's predictions in a controlled setting.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Looped transformers are a parameter-efficient variant of standard transformers where the same set of layers (or a single block) is applied iteratively over the same latent representation, often called recurrent depth or looped depth sharing. This design promises test-time compute scaling: spending more iterations on harder problems. Fixed-point iteration is a phenomenon where hidden states stabilize after several layers, and analyzing it helps understand when iterative computation yields meaningful predictions. Layer normalization, especially its placement (e.g., pre-LN, post-LN, outer normalization), is known to critically affect training stability and gradient propagation in transformers.

## [Bidirectional Manifold Consistency for Self-Verification in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16565) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Bidirectional Manifold Consistency (BMC), a training-free, unsupervised metric that quantifies the validity of reasoning traces in diffusion large language models (dLLMs) by measuring stability under a forward-masking and backward-reconstruction cycle. BMC is shown to be effective for diagnosis without ground truth, rejection resampling during inference, and as a dense geometric reward for alignment. This work offers a novel geometric perspective on self-verification for dLLMs, potentially improving reasoning reliability and enabling efficient use of computational resources by concentrating on complex tasks. It could impact how dLLMs are evaluated and aligned, especially in scenarios where ground truth is unavailable. BMC is training-free and unsupervised, leveraging the hypothesis that valid reasoning trajectories lie on a high-density manifold as stable attractors, while invalid ones drift off-manifold. The method requires a forward-masking and backward-reconstruction cycle, and its effectiveness is demonstrated across diagnosis, inference, and alignment stages.

## [Heterogeneous Chain-of-Thought Supervision: When Efficient Learning Is Possible](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24737) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces learning-theoretic models of heterogeneous chain-of-thought (CoT) supervision from multiple sources, each providing correct traces with the same final answer but different intermediate steps. It proves cryptographic hardness of learning from passively collected traces from multiple sources, even when learning from homogeneous CoTs is easy, and gives an efficient algorithm that actively and adaptively collects CoTs with a per-source budget independent of the target error ε. This work offers a theoretical explanation for why curating CoT supervision matters: heterogeneity across sources can make learning computationally hard, so data collection and filtering practices directly affect whether CoT's computational advantage is realized. It provides guidance for researchers and practitioners building CoT datasets for LLM reasoning training. The hardness can stem from hard-to-learn contribution biases that determine which sources contribute traces on different parts of the domain, or from receiving an unfiltered heterogeneous mixture, though a filtered subset of homogeneous traces suffices for efficient learning. The positive result is inspired by iterative and adaptive fine-tuning of language models, where active collection avoids the hardness of passive learning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) supervision trains language models to produce intermediate reasoning steps before a final answer, which makes some computationally hard learning problems tractable via next-token prediction. In practice, CoT datasets are assembled from heterogeneous sources with different reasoning styles, and substantial effort goes into curating them for next-token training. Learning theory studies the sample and computational complexity of learning algorithms, and this paper asks when heterogeneous CoT supervision remains efficiently learnable.

## [ReGuard finds and guards worst-case scenarios for RL network controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04373) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ReGuard, a new framework, discovers worst-case network conditions for RL-based controllers via bilevel regret maximization and compiles counterfactual trajectories into lightweight logic rules for inference-time protection without retraining. Evaluated on Pensieve, Sage, and Park, it uncovers 43-64% performance losses and shrinks these gaps by 79-85% in the first iteration. This addresses a critical robustness gap in RL-based network controllers, which often excel on average but fail catastrophically under specific conditions. The rule-based runtime protection offers a practical, retraining-free safeguard that could make RL deployments in congestion control and adaptive bitrate streaming more reliable. ReGuard certifies a lower bound on the worst-case performance gap through bilevel regret maximization, and its protection extends beyond discovered scenarios, improving performance across a wide range of network conditions. The approach avoids formal verification, which is impractical for sequential closed-loop RL controllers.

## [Variable Codebook Size Quantization Tackles Entropy Cliff in Visual Tokenizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06207) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper identifies an 'Entropy Cliff' in discrete visual tokenizers, where per-position conditional entropy decays rapidly, and proposes Variable Codebook Size Quantization (VCQ) that grows codebook size monotonically along the sequence from K_min=2 to K_max. VCQ-Base achieves gFID 14.80 without CFG on ImageNet 256x256, compared to 27.98 for a constant codebook of size 16384, and scaled up reaches gFID 1.71 with 684M parameters. This work challenges the default constant-codebook design in discrete visual tokenizers, showing that how codebook capacity is distributed along the sequence matters as much as total capacity. It could influence future tokenizer design for autoregressive image generation, potentially improving efficiency and quality without changing the loss function or training procedure. The extreme information bottleneck at K_min=2 encourages semantic concentration in early encoder features, as a linear probe on features from only the first 10 positions reaches 43.8% top-1 accuracy on ImageNet versus 27.1% for uniform codebooks. The method leaves the loss function, parameter count, and AR training procedure unchanged, and the scaled-up result requires no extra techniques like semantic regularization or causal alignment.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Discrete visual tokenizers map images to sequences of tokens from a learned codebook, which are then modeled by autoregressive Transformers for image generation. Typically, every position in the sequence shares the same codebook size, but this paper shows that conditional entropy drops sharply after a few positions, making most prefixes uniquely identify images. This 'Entropy Cliff' means larger codebooks exhaust the finite-data entropy budget quickly, motivating adaptive codebook sizing.

## [UniSD: A Unified Framework for Self-Distillation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06597) ⭐️ 7.0/10

UniSD introduces a unified framework that systematically studies self-distillation for large language models by integrating multi-teacher agreement, EMA teacher stabilization, token-level contrastive learning, feature matching, and divergence clipping. Evaluated across six benchmarks and six models from three model families, the integrated UniSDfull pipeline improves over the base model by +5.4 points and the strongest baseline by +2.8 points. This work addresses a real gap in the literature where self-distillation design choices were studied in isolation, providing a comprehensive ablation-style analysis of when and why self-distillation improves over static imitation. It highlights self-distillation as a practical and steerable approach for efficient LLM adaptation without relying on stronger external teachers, which is valuable for ML researchers and practitioners. The framework reveals which components drive gains and how they interact across tasks, but the abstract does not specify the exact benchmarks, model sizes, or computational overhead. The approach focuses on autoregressive LLMs where self-generated trajectories are free-form and correctness is task-dependent, making supervision reliability a key challenge.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Self-distillation (SD) is a technique where a model learns from its own outputs or from a teacher derived from itself, avoiding the need for a separate, stronger external teacher model. In autoregressive LLMs, SD is challenging because the model generates free-form text and plausible rationales may still provide unstable or unreliable supervision. Existing methods often examine isolated design choices, leaving their effectiveness and interactions unclear. UniSD systematically integrates complementary mechanisms to address supervision reliability, representation alignment, and training stability.

<a id="item-233"></a>
## [Thalamus-Reasoning: 9B Multimodal LLM for Multi-Sample Omics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06728) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Thalamus-Reasoning is a 9B multimodal large language model that encodes bulk or single-cell transcriptomes as compact continuous embeddings interleaved with text, enabling multi-sample omics reasoning. It was trained on a purpose-built corpus spanning 157 task groups, with continued pretraining on 20 billion tokens and supervised fine-tuning on 5.21 million conversations, and introduces the GEO-OmicsQA benchmark of 3,000 publication-disjoint questions. This work shows that quantitative transcriptomic measurements can be integrated directly into a general conversational LLM, moving beyond lossy text-only gene lists and narrow task-specific encoders. It could enable language-guided interpretation and comparison of experimental samples, benefiting bioinformatics and AI-for-science research. Thalamus-Reasoning achieves the best results on three of four held-out annotation tasks and all six GEO-OmicsQA variants, and the best average performance on PerturbQA perturbation-response prediction; ablations confirm a clear advantage of continuous omics representations over text-only gene lists. The model is 9B parameters and handles both bulk and single-cell transcriptomes within the same context as natural-language instructions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Transcriptomics measures gene expression across thousands of genes, producing high-dimensional quantitative profiles that are difficult for standard LLMs to ingest. Bulk RNA-seq captures population-averaged expression, while single-cell RNA-seq resolves expression at individual cell resolution. Multimodal LLMs can interleave different data types—such as continuous embeddings and text—within a single context, enabling reasoning over heterogeneous inputs.
