A new arXiv paper (2611.07720) proposes using large language models purely as feature extractors for zero-shot multivariate time-series anomaly detection, rather than optimizing surrogate objectives like forecasting or reconstruction. It evaluates feature separability under an isolation assumption using step-wise tokenization (one time-step per token) and an unsupervised Isolation Forest, and also investigates whether performance stems from language pre-training. This work challenges the common reliance on surrogate objectives in LLM-based time-series anomaly detection, arguing that highly expressive models can reconstruct or forecast anomalies and thus undermine those strategies. If validated, it could shift research toward direct representation analysis and broaden applications of cross-modal foundation models. The method uses simple step-wise tokenization with one time-step per token to preserve fine-grained local temporal and variable structure, and assesses representation quality via an unsupervised Isolation Forest. The study also examines whether observed performance can be attributed to language-related pre-training or arises independently of it.

Recent work adapts LLMs to time series via cross-modal transfer, but existing approaches are poorly suited to multivariate TSAD because tokenization often obscures fine-grained temporal and variable structures, and most methods optimize auxiliary objectives such as forecasting or reconstruction. ## [Token Identity Brier Flow Improves Continuous Language Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07721) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes Token Identity Brier Flow (TIBF), a method that applies Brier loss to token probabilities for continued training of pretrained continuous language flow models. With frozen embeddings, TIBF achieves Gen.PPL of 33.96 on OWT and 81.17 on LM1B, outperforming continuous-MSE and cross-entropy continuation controls. This work addresses a known limitation of continuous MSE supervision in flow-based language models, where different probability distributions can yield the same expected embedding and become indistinguishable. It offers a principled alternative that could improve the quality and diversity of text generated by continuous diffusion and flow models. TIBF combines network scores with token-matching scores under a Gaussian observation model and uses probability-weighted embeddings for generation. The authors prove that the squared error between predicted token probabilities and the true posterior bounds the squared errors of continuous denoising predictions and flow velocities, and they establish convergence conditions under ideal and error-prone predictions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Diffusion and flow models have recently been extended to language modeling by generating text through continuous denoising in token embedding spaces. These models map discrete tokens to continuous embeddings and learn a flow that transports noise to data, but fine-tuning them with limited additional training requires an appropriate connection between supervision and continuous generation. The Brier score is a proper scoring rule that measures the mean squared difference between predicted probabilities and actual outcomes, and it is commonly used in classification and probabilistic forecasting.

## [O²PD: Overlap-Gated On-Policy Distillation on Reliable Prefix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07731) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes O²PD, an overlap-gated on-policy distillation method that applies alignment only to the first 20% of tokens in each student rollout and uses an overlap-gated α-divergence to interpolate between mode-covering and mode-seeking behavior. It also introduces Reset Support and Support Convergence strategies to address the Teacher-support Uncovering and Dominant-Mode Mismatch problems in top-k OPD. This work improves the reliability of token-level teacher supervision in on-policy distillation for LLMs, potentially leading to more efficient and effective distillation of large models into smaller ones. It addresses key gaps in existing top-k OPD approaches, which are widely used but suffer from off-policy teacher scoring and discarded probability mass. The method restricts alignment to the 20% prefix of each student rollout based on quantitative analysis showing higher reliability there, and theoretically characterizes two problems of top-k OPD: Teacher-support Uncovering and Dominant-Mode Mismatch. The overlap-gated α-divergence adaptively switches support and modulates α per token, with Reset Support pooling mass outside teacher top-k and Support Convergence converging to the aligned intersection.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on its own generated trajectories, using a teacher model to provide dense token-level supervision, which helps the student correct its own mistakes in its own state space. Existing OPD methods often use top-k truncation of the teacher's distribution and apply reverse Kullback-Leibler divergence between renormalized distributions, but this can be unreliable because the teacher scores student-generated rollouts that are off-policy for itself, and it discards probability mass outside the top-k support.

## [LLM token probabilities are overconfident for human behavior simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07735) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv case study evaluates Ministral 3 (3B and 8B) on a Next Like Prediction task using data from Luxembourgish news platform RTL.lu, finding that with in-context learning only, the models are overconfident and less accurate than a simple statistical baseline. Scaling from 3B to 8B improves accuracy but degrades calibration, while task-specific supervised fine-tuning reduces Expected Calibration Error from 0.2 to 0.006 and raises accuracy from 0.536 to 0.758. This finding challenges the common practice of evaluating LLM-based human behavior simulations by accuracy alone, showing that confidence estimates can be dangerously miscalibrated even as models grow larger. It matters for researchers and practitioners deploying LLM agents in social simulation, recommendation, and user modeling, where reliable uncertainty is essential for valid conclusions. The study uses only in-context learning without fine-tuning, and finds that longer behavioral histories raise accuracy while confidence declines, indicating that accuracy and calibration do not move together. The supervised fine-tuning intervention is task-specific, so its calibration benefit may not generalize to other tasks or domains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used as agents to simulate human behavior, such as predicting user preferences from digital traces. Calibration refers to how well a model's predicted confidence matches its actual accuracy—for example, if a model says it is 70% confident, it should be correct about 70% of the time. Token probabilities are the model's internal likelihood scores for each possible next token, often used as confidence estimates. In-context learning means providing examples in the prompt without updating model weights, while supervised fine-tuning trains the model on task-specific labeled data.

Researchers introduce GenVer, a generative verifier that scores knowledge graph triples by combining their serialized form with generated natural language representations (NLRs). On four FactFB variants derived from FB15k, GenVer achieves the highest classification accuracy among evaluated baselines, ranging from 88.56% to 91.52%. This work shows that generated natural language representations can effectively complement structural information for triple classification, potentially improving knowledge graph quality assessment. The approach bridges graph-based and text-based methods, offering a new direction for semantic accuracy verification in knowledge graphs. GenVer uses a triple-text alignment procedure to select training pairs for a sequence-to-sequence generator, and a BERT-based classifier then combines features from each triple and its generated NLR. Case analysis reveals limitations such as semantic drift and high-confidence errors.

## [NEDNet decomposes network effects for causal inference under interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07879) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces NEDNet, a network effect decomposition method that models the conditional outcome response as a pre-treatment baseline plus separate treatment- and exposure-response pathways, rather than a single joint predictor. Experiments on semi-synthetic benchmarks from BlogCatalog and Flickr show NEDNet achieves the lowest PEHE for individual, peer, and total effects across homogeneous and covariate-heterogeneous settings. This work addresses a key challenge in causal inference under network interference: separating direct treatment effects from spillover effects of neighborhood exposure. Improved estimation of these distinct pathways can benefit fields like social science, epidemiology, and economics where observational network data are common. NEDNet uses path-specific neighborhood contextualization to provide local pre-treatment information to each pathway and characterizes the fitted response via a unique reference-normalized decomposition equivalent to a zero mixed treatment-exposure contrast. Validation is limited to semi-synthetic benchmarks, so real-world performance remains to be tested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Causal inference under network interference deals with settings where an individual's outcome depends not only on their own treatment but also on the treatments of others in their network. Traditional methods often model treatment and exposure jointly, making it hard to interpret the separate response pathways. BlogCatalog and Flickr are social network datasets commonly used to generate semi-synthetic benchmarks for such methods.

<a id="item-336"></a>
## [Active In-Context Learning for Tabular Foundation Models: Diversity Wins Early](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07886) ⭐️ 6.0/10

This paper introduces Target-Aware Nearest Neighbor (TANN) acquisition and Expected Predictive Information Gain in Context (EPIC) for active in-context learning with tabular foundation models (TFMs), and evaluates them against classical acquisition rules across 20 benchmarks using two TFMs and gradient-boosted decision trees (GBDTs). The study finds that diversity-based acquisition is best during the first acquisition iterations, that labeling pool rows nearest to target inputs via TANN matters substantially, and that EPIC yields the best models at the label budget, retaining 80–87% of chance-corrected full-data performance with only 5–10% of the pool labeled on the eight largest-pool benchmarks. TFMs adapt to new tasks only through labeled rows in their context, so reducing label cost directly affects how practical these models are in domains where expert annotation is expensive. This work provides concrete guidance on when, where, and how to acquire labels for TFMs, and shows that labels selected for a TFM can also improve GBDT performance, suggesting transferable value across model families. The study compares diversity, predictive uncertainty, and target-aware information gain strategies, with TANN using a simple nearest-neighbor rule to prioritize pool rows serving target inputs and EPIC measuring expected predictive information gain in context. Results hold across two well-established TFMs and classical GBDTs on 20 benchmarks, though the paper is an empirical study and the abstract does not report absolute performance levels or computational overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Tabular foundation models (TFMs), such as TabPFN, are pretrained models that make predictions on spreadsheet-like data in a single forward pass without task-specific training, and they adapt to new tasks via in-context learning from labeled rows provided in their context. Active learning traditionally selects the most informative unlabeled examples to label, but in the in-context setting the model guiding acquisition changes with every label added, making classical acquisition rules less directly applicable. This paper studies active in-context learning for TFMs, asking how the value of different acquisition strategies evolves as a frozen TFM accumulates more context.

<a id="item-337"></a>
## [DISCERN: Distributional Synthetic Replay for Retain-Free Incremental Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07892) ⭐️ 6.0/10

DISCERN introduces an intervention-based incremental class unlearning method that replaces retain-set access with synthetic embeddings sampled from per-class parametric distributions, selected via statistical tests and cross-validated log-likelihood. Evaluated on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100 with convolutional and transformer backbones, it achieves a harmonic mean of 0.797 on ViT-B/16 after 20 sequential deletions, while the strongest simultaneous-setting baseline drops from 0.918 to 0.048. This work addresses a key challenge in real-world machine unlearning: deletion requests arrive over time, requiring incremental unlearning without access to the original retain set. By eliminating the need for retain data and showing strong stability on transformer architectures, DISCERN could enable more practical privacy-compliant model maintenance in deployed systems. DISCERN fits both Euclidean (Gaussian, Laplace, Student's t) and directional (von Mises-Fisher, Fisher-Bingham) distributions to each class, motivated by the observation that transformer embeddings lie approximately on a hypersphere and concentrate around class directions. A paired ablation shows that per-class distribution selection substantially reduces the resurfacing of previously forgotten classes, acting as a stabilizer of accumulated forgetting rather than a uniform improvement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of specific training data from a trained model without full retraining. Incremental class unlearning (ICU) extends this to scenarios where classes are removed sequentially, requiring that previously forgotten knowledge stays forgotten while remaining classes are preserved. Traditional methods often rely on a retain set (data from remaining classes) to maintain performance, but this may not be available in practice. DISCERN uses synthetic replay from fitted distributions to avoid retain-set access, leveraging statistical distributions like von Mises-Fisher for directional data on spheres.

## [CLEAR-WSI: Scalable Diagnostic-Driven Retrieval for Pathology Archives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07935) ⭐️ 6.0/10

CLEAR-WSI is a new framework that uses fixed-length slide embeddings to retrieve similar whole-slide pathology images, refining candidates with predicted or ground-truth diagnostic labels and adding new cases to an expanding memory bank. On a benchmark of two public and one private clinical datasets across four retrieval tasks, it improves Precision@5 over the strongest baseline by 3.10 on CAMELYON16, 2.75 on BRACS-1, 1.95 on BRACS-2, and 3.75 on X-HER2. This work addresses a real scalability bottleneck in digital pathology archives, where inconsistent or missing metadata makes content-based retrieval difficult. By enabling efficient, diagnostic-aware retrieval without relying on manual annotations, it could help pathologists find similar cases faster and support clinical decision-making and research. The method uses a constant-length embedding for each whole-slide image, avoiding handcrafted indexing pipelines and predefined sampling strategies, and it supports both label-predicted and ground-truth retrieval settings. Ablation and latency studies are included, though the abstract does not report absolute latency numbers or discuss deployment constraints in detail.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Whole-slide images (WSIs) are digitized glass slides produced by robotic microscopes that stitch many small images into a single high-resolution file, and hematoxylin-and-eosin (H&E) staining is the most common routine stain in pathology. Content-based image retrieval (CBIR) finds similar images directly from visual content rather than text metadata, which is useful when annotations are inconsistent or unavailable. However, WSIs are extremely large, so existing retrieval systems often rely on patch-based processing and handcrafted pipelines that limit scalability.

## [Gradient Boosting Eliminates Rank Search in Tensor Network Kernel Machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08093) ⭐️ 6.0/10

GB-CPKM is a gradient boosting framework that incrementally builds fixed-rank CP tensor network kernel machines, avoiding explicit rank search. On real-world regression benchmarks, it trains up to 6x faster and uses up to 2x less peak memory than cross-validation-based rank selection while achieving comparable predictive performance. Rank selection is a major bottleneck in tensor network kernel machines, requiring training multiple candidate models. GB-CPKM's incremental capacity approach could make these models more scalable and practical for large-scale regression tasks, benefiting specialists in tensor methods and kernel machines. The method uses a canonical polyadic (CP) tensor network parameterization and sequentially adds fixed-rank learners, with ensemble size chosen by validation performance instead of explicit rank search. Experiments show comparable predictive performance to cross-validation-based rank selection, but the approach is limited to regression benchmarks and CP decomposition.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Tensor network kernel machines (TNKMs) combine nonlinear feature maps with low-rank tensor network representations to efficiently model complex relationships. The canonical polyadic (CP) decomposition factorizes a tensor into a sum of rank-one tensors, and its rank controls model capacity. Gradient boosting is an ensemble technique that sequentially adds weak learners to build a strong predictor, typically using decision trees, but here adapted to fixed-rank tensor learners.

## [DART disentangles domain residuals for generalist anomaly detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08271) ⭐️ 6.0/10

DART introduces a plug-in framework for normal-abnormal guided generalist anomaly detection that separates residuals into anomaly-relevant and domain-specific components using a Residual Disentanglement Module and Cross-Domain Meta-Training. Experiments on MVTec AD, VisA, and BraTS show consistent improvements over multiple base methods, with the largest gains under stronger domain shifts. This work addresses a key limitation in cross-domain anomaly detection—residual entanglement—by making the separation architecture-agnostic and insertable into existing pipelines, potentially improving generalization in industrial inspection and medical imaging without retraining. The method is architecture-agnostic and can be inserted between residual computation and the detection head of existing GAD pipelines; it is evaluated on MVTec AD, VisA, and BraTS, with the largest gains under stronger domain shifts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Generalist Anomaly Detection (GAD) aims to detect anomalies in unseen domains using only a few reference samples, unlike traditional methods that require training a separate model per dataset. Normal-abnormal guided GAD improves this by using both normal and anomalous references, but the residuals often mix transferable anomaly cues with domain-specific appearance factors, limiting cross-domain transfer.

## [Hybrid LSTM-XGBoost Framework Predicts Rare Landing Safety Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08386) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.08386) proposes an interpretable hybrid framework that fuses LSTM autoencoder reconstruction errors with statistical flight-history features and feeds them into a class-weighted XGBoost classifier to predict landing-phase safety events. Evaluated on 53,750 NASA DASHlink approach windows from three airports, it achieves mean macro recall above 0.90 across all airports under repeated partitioning and retraining. Rare landing-phase anomalies are hard to detect in strongly imbalanced flight data, and this work shows that combining deep temporal reconstruction with gradient-boosted classification can deliver high recall while remaining interpretable. It offers practical guidance for adapting flight-data monitoring models to airport-level data availability, which could support aviation safety monitoring systems. The LSTM autoencoder is trained only on nominal flight windows, and its continuous reconstruction-error descriptor is fused with parameter-specific statistical features; component comparisons show that class weighting is essential and continuous fusion outperforms reconstruction-error-only and hard-gate alternatives. Airport-level comparisons reveal benefits from local specialization at the two larger airports and from pooled training at the smallest airport, with feature attributions linking predictions to vertical-path, configuration, speed, and approach-context variables.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: An LSTM autoencoder is a neural network trained to reconstruct normal time-series windows; at inference, anomalous inputs produce high reconstruction error, which serves as an anomaly score. XGBoost is a gradient-boosted tree library that supports class weighting (e.g., scale_pos_weight) to handle imbalanced classification. NASA DASHlink is a public repository of flight-recorded data with hundreds of parameters per flight, often used for flight-data mining and anomaly detection research.

## [Label-Wording Augmentation Helps Unfamiliar Words, Not Abbreviations or Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08439) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper compares two releases of Ru-Jev, a 307M-parameter multilingual cross-encoder trained on thirteen Russian-language tasks, where the later release adds label-wording augmentation that presents labels in multiple surface forms. Testing ten label vocabularies with five and eighteen candidates per question, the augmented model improves accuracy by up to 0.24 on held-out vocabularies of ordinary or borrowed words, but shows no usable gain on abbreviations or opaque codes. This work highlights a key limitation of typed-decision models: their accuracy depends on label wording supplied at request time, which developers cannot control. The finding that augmentation helps with unfamiliar words but not abbreviations or codes informs practitioners about where such robustness techniques are effective and where they fail, affecting NLP system design for multilingual and low-resource settings. Eleven of fourteen gains survive Holm correction, with exceptions for slang with eighteen candidates and descriptive sentences with five; on abbreviations the augmented release is not better and worse on the clean slice, and on opaque codes both releases stay at or below chance once training-related labels are removed. In most cells where accuracy rises the risk-coverage area falls, but with eighteen candidates confident errors rise on jargon and slang, and on two unseen datasets accuracy moves by -8.4 and +5.6 points.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Typed-decision models are encoders that score a set of options supplied by the caller at inference time, returning a typed answer such as a category or a choice among up to 255 options. Because the label wording is not fixed during training, the model must generalize to surface forms it has not seen. Label-wording augmentation trains the model on multiple paraphrases or surface forms of each label to improve robustness. A cross-encoder is a model that jointly encodes a question and a candidate label to produce a relevance score, commonly used in reranking.

## [ProMoral-Bench compares 10 prompting strategies for LLM moral reasoning and safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13274) ⭐️ 6.0/10

ProMoral-Bench systematically evaluates 10 prompting paradigms across four LLM families using ETHICS, Scruples, WildJailbreak, and a new 200-pair ETHICS-Contrast set, introducing composite metrics MCS, SRS, and UMSS. It finds that compact exemplar-guided prompts achieve better competence-robustness trade-offs at lower token cost than the tested multi-stage variants. Prompt design significantly affects LLM moral judgments and refusal behavior, yet controlled comparisons have been fragmented; this benchmark provides a standardized framework for evaluating the safety-usability trade-off. The findings can guide practitioners in choosing prompting strategies that balance moral competence with jailbreak robustness and over-refusal avoidance. UMSS is a study-specific relative summary of MCS and SRS, not an absolute measure of model safety or moral grounding. The few-shot demonstrations are non-overlapping but drawn from the evaluation distributions, so their advantage should not be interpreted as out-of-distribution robustness.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used in morally sensitive applications, and their outputs depend heavily on how prompts are crafted. Jailbreak attacks try to bypass safety filters to elicit harmful responses, while over-refusal occurs when models reject benign queries, creating a safety-usability trade-off. Benchmarks like ETHICS evaluate moral reasoning across domains such as commonsense morality and justice, but comparing prompting strategies across models and datasets has been challenging.

## [AAC: Differentiable Landmark Compression for ALT Heuristics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.20744) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces the Architecturally Admissible Compressor (AAC), which provides two continuous relaxations of ALT landmark selection to adapt heuristics to query demand under a fixed label-memory budget. It proves that every parameter value yields a lower bound on shortest-path distance, and that the relaxed and discrete optima coincide, enabling a continuous greedy algorithm (AAC-CG) with exact gradients and an entropy-regularized oracle. This work bridges differentiable optimization and heuristic search, offering a principled way to compress landmark sets while preserving admissibility, which is critical for A* speedups. It could benefit practitioners in road-network routing and other pathfinding applications where memory is constrained. At matched label memory, AAC-CG reduces A* expansions by 1.9–22.7% relative to farthest-point ALT (FPS-ALT) across 19 graph-budget combinations on six road networks and two synthetic graphs, achieving 99.6% of canonical greedy's mean heuristic value. The limiting 1−1/e approximation factor is optimal for arbitrary supplied pools and query distributions unless P=NP.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: The ALT algorithm speeds up A* shortest-path queries by selecting a set of landmark nodes and using the triangle inequality to compute lower-bound distances from any node to the goal. These lower bounds serve as admissible heuristics, which never overestimate the true cost, ensuring A* finds optimal paths. AAC introduces a differentiable relaxation of landmark selection to optimize heuristic quality under memory constraints.

## [ProCompNav Uses Binary Comparative Questions to Resolve Ambiguous Navigation Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06223) ⭐️ 6.0/10

ProCompNav introduces a two-stage framework for interactive instance navigation that first builds a candidate pool and then uses Recursive Comparative Judgment (RCJ) to identify the target. RCJ iteratively selects an attribute-value pair that splits the candidate pool, asks the user a single binary yes/no question, and prunes all inconsistent candidates at once, without needing an attribute unique to the target. This work addresses a key human-robot interaction problem: resolving ambiguous user requests with minimal user burden. By replacing open-ended questions with efficient binary comparisons, it could make assistive and service robots more practical in cluttered real-world environments. ProCompNav is training-free and substantially improves Success Rate while reducing Response Length on an interactive navigation benchmark, and achieves the highest Success Rate among evaluated methods on a non-interactive benchmark. Two human studies found that participants preferred its binary questions over open-ended questions and its batched delivery over interleaved interaction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Interactive instance navigation requires a robot to find a specific object among similar distractors in an unknown 3D environment by asking the user for clarification. Existing approaches typically ask open-ended questions and pick the candidate that best matches accumulated user information, which can lead to long responses and premature decisions. Recursive Comparative Judgment is a strategy that repeatedly splits the candidate set with targeted yes/no questions until only the intended target remains.

## [PRISE and NeurPRISE: Problem-Driven Scenario Reduction for Robust Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14494) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces PRISE, a problem-driven sequential lookahead heuristic that constructs reduced scenario sets by evaluating the marginal impact of each scenario, and NeurPRISE, a GNN-Transformer surrogate trained via imitation learning with a gain-aware ranking objective to accelerate PRISE. Experiments on three two-stage robust optimization problems show NeurPRISE achieves competitive regret and delivers 17-200x speedup over PRISE, with strong zero-shot generalization to larger scales and distribution shifts. Scenario reduction is critical for making two-stage robust optimization tractable, and existing methods are largely problem-agnostic; PRISE's problem-driven approach and NeurPRISE's neural acceleration could significantly improve scalability for real-world applications. The 17-200x speedup and zero-shot generalization make this approach promising for large-scale optimization problems in operations research and machine learning. NeurPRISE uses graph convolution to encode per-scenario structure and attention to capture cross-scenario interactions, and is trained via imitation learning with a gain-aware ranking objective that distills marginal gain information from PRISE. It maintains strong scalability with varying numbers of scenarios and handles instances with up to 5x larger problem scales and 4x more scenarios in zero-shot settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Two-stage robust optimization (2RO) with discrete uncertainty involves making decisions in two stages to hedge against uncertainty, but exact solutions are often computationally prohibitive. Scenario reduction selects a small representative subset of scenarios to make the problem tractable, but traditional methods are problem-agnostic and ignore the feasible region and recourse structure. Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and Transformers are deep learning architectures adept at modeling relational and sequential data, respectively, and are increasingly used as surrogate models to accelerate expensive optimization procedures.

The paper proposes TSQAgent, an agentic reasoning framework with three collaborative roles—Perceiver, Inspector, and Adjudicator—for rating time series data quality, and introduces TSQBench, a benchmark to evaluate LLMs on identifying relevant quality dimensions and performing grounded comparisons. Current LLMs struggle with both dimension identification and evidence-grounded quality comparison, so this work addresses a gap in LLM-based time series quality assessment and shows that improved quality rating can lead to better quality-aware data selection and downstream performance. TSQAgent uses external analytical tools for precise quantitative comparisons over selected dimensions, and experiments on TSQBench and eleven real-world datasets demonstrate substantial improvements in quality understanding and comparison, translating into enhanced downstream performance and data efficiency.

## [ThermoLLM: LLM-based HVAC control with spatiotemporal context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22911) ⭐️ 6.0/10

ThermoLLM is a new LLM-based HVAC control framework that uses a spatial knowledge graph derived from Brick-style building semantics, linked with recent observations from a rolling history, to ground the LLM's context. In a five-zone building across six climate-season scenarios, it achieved the best energy-comfort trade-offs and outperformed all baselines in TOPSIS rankings. This work addresses key limitations of prior LLM-based HVAC controllers, such as inconsistent results, poor generalization, and control latency, by centering the LLM context on building semantics and dynamics. It could lead to more reliable and energy-efficient control in smart buildings, benefiting building managers and occupants. The framework uses a spatial knowledge graph from Brick-style building semantics and a rolling history of observations to provide spatiotemporal context. Evaluation was conducted in a simulated five-zone building across six climate-season scenarios, comparing against standard control methods and recent state-of-the-art LLM-based baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Multi-zone HVAC control is a spatial decision problem where thermal dynamics depend on zone layout, adjacency, and delayed interactions. Recent LLM-based controllers have shown feasibility but struggle with consistency and generalization. ThermoLLM introduces building semantics and spatiotemporal context to improve performance.

## [DiffDiff embeds predictability asymmetry into diffusion trajectory for time series forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22599) ⭐️ 6.0/10

DiffDiff introduces a diffusion framework that makes the forward process step-dependent, progressively shifting the noisy state from the target toward its second-order differenced structure, while a stage-adaptive gate balances value-domain and differential history conditioning. On seven benchmarks across four prediction horizons, it outperforms six diffusion baselines. This work addresses a core inefficiency in diffusion-based forecasting by encoding the asymmetry between slowly-varying, history-anchored dynamics and high-frequency uncertainty directly into the corruption trajectory, potentially leading to more accurate and distributionally faithful probabilistic forecasts. It could influence future designs of diffusion models for time series and other sequential data. The terminal distribution approaches a standard Gaussian, preserving compatibility with existing samplers, and analyses show the trajectory reallocates modeling effort according to history predictability. However, the approach is specialized and evaluated only on standard benchmarks without community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Diffusion models generate data by reversing a gradual noise-adding process and have become popular for probabilistic time series forecasting, where the goal is to model the distribution of future values given history. In time series, the future continues the observed past, so slowly-varying components are largely determined by continuity while higher-frequency components carry most uncertainty; standard diffusion processes treat all parts equally during corruption.

## [LFRA: Local-Frame Rotary Attention for Antibody-Specific Epitope Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01092) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes Local-Frame Rotary Attention (LFRA), which encodes inter-residue 3D displacements in backbone-defined local frames directly into rotary attention to improve antibody-specific epitope prediction. The code and dataset are available at https://github.com/better-fcl/lfra. This work introduces a novel positional mechanism that injects folded residue geometry into attention, potentially improving the accuracy of antibody-specific epitope prediction. It could benefit structural biology and machine learning research by offering a more geometrically grounded alternative to sequence-based positional encodings. LFRA expresses inter-residue displacements in backbone-defined local frames and injects them directly into rotary attention, preserving continuous directional geometry while ensuring invariance to independent SE(3) transformations. The method is evaluated on antibody-specific epitope prediction, with code and dataset released on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Antibody-specific epitope prediction aims to identify which antigen residues are recognized by a given antibody, relying on the 3D complementarity between antibody CDRs and the antigen surface. Existing methods often use protein language model embeddings and inject structure via graph, surface, or point-cloud encoders, while the positional mechanism inside attention remains tied to 1D sequence order. Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) is a widely used technique in transformers that encodes position by rotating query and key vectors, but it is typically applied to sequence positions rather than 3D geometry.

## [Compliance-First Agentic AI Architecture Proposed for Hospitals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06112) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.06112v2) proposes a hospital-specific, compliance-first agentic AI architecture that extends existing hospital AI platform models with three additions: an Agent Orchestration Layer for multi-agent workflows across clinical, operational, and financial domains, a Compliance and Policy Layer that centralizes policy-as-code for HIPAA, GDPR, the EU AI Act, DISHA, India's DPDP Act, and ISO/IEC standards, and a Privacy-Preserving Data Fabric integrating federated learning, differential privacy, and secure enclaves into HIMS flows. Using a synthetic but structurally realistic hospital dataset and an open, ready-to-deploy prototype, the authors demonstrate end-to-end orchestration of triage risk prediction, workflow optimization, and compliance logging with simulated reductions in task turnaround time and manual documentation effort. An estimated 70-80% of healthcare AI pilots fail to scale, largely due to governance gaps, fragmented data, and missing integration blueprints, so a concrete architectural blueprint that treats compliance as a first-class layer could help hospital leaders move from isolated point solutions to governed, ROI-focused platforms. The proposal is relevant to healthcare AI vendors, hospital IT and compliance teams, and regulators navigating overlapping frameworks such as HIPAA, GDPR, the EU AI Act, DISHA, and India's DPDP Act. The work is a conceptual and architectural proposal rather than an empirical breakthrough: validation relies on a synthetic but structurally realistic hospital dataset and simulated results, so real-world performance, cost, and regulatory acceptance remain untested. The prototype is described as open and ready-to-deploy, and the architecture is designed to be tailored to on-premise, hybrid, and cloud-native deployments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Hospitals are adopting AI for triage, imaging, and scheduling, but most deployments remain isolated point solutions locked inside departmental silos, causing duplicated effort and hidden risks. Policy-as-code is an approach that encodes regulatory and organizational rules as machine-enforceable code, often integrated into CI/CD pipelines and enforced by tools such as Open Policy Agent, so compliance can be validated continuously rather than through manual interpretation. DISHA (Digital Information Security in Healthcare Act) is India's proposed health data protection law, which overlaps with the DPDP Act, 2023 and is often compared to HIPAA; it has not yet been formally adopted. Multi-agent orchestration refers to patterns for coordinating multiple AI agents, including handoffs and human-in-the-loop approvals, to deliver modular and scalable workflows.

## [Learner2Skill externalizes LLM learner simulation into reusable skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37157) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.37157v3) proposes Learner2Skill, a method that externalizes the simulation capability learned from a learner's historical interactions into a persistent, evolving 'Simulation Skill' rather than reprocessing the full interaction history each time. The Skill can be transferred to a new LLM through lightweight executor calibration, and experiments show it reproduces fine-grained learner behavior more faithfully while reducing overall token cost. Learner simulation is increasingly used in education technology to test tutoring systems and generate synthetic student data, but repeated context reconstruction makes LLM-based simulators expensive and ties them to a single model. By making the learner profile a portable, reusable artifact, Learner2Skill could lower inference costs and let developers switch or upgrade LLM backends without rebuilding simulations from scratch. The Skill captures the learner's current learning state and recurring response patterns and evolves as new real interactions arrive; adapting it to a different LLM requires only lightweight executor calibration instead of reconstructing the learner from scratch. The abstract is truncated in the listing, and the reported gains are domain-specific to learner simulation rather than general-purpose.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: LLM-based learner simulation uses a large language model to imitate how a specific student would respond to new tasks, which is useful for evaluating educational interventions and generating realistic data. Existing approaches typically feed the learner's growing interaction history into the model's context each time, incurring rising token costs and making the learned simulation hard to reuse across different LLMs. Learner2Skill borrows the idea of 'skills' as persistent, transferable artifacts, separating the learner representation from the model that executes it.

## [VIGOR: Zero-Shot Visual Generalization via Latent Consistency in MBRL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02801) ⭐️ 6.0/10

VIGOR introduces asymmetric weak-to-strong augmentation and dynamics-level latent consistency to achieve zero-shot visual generalization in model-based reinforcement learning. Instantiated on TD-MPC2, it outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by 3.4% on DeepMind Control Suite and 43.6% on Robosuite while maintaining sample efficiency comparable to its backbone. This work addresses a critical weakness of model-based RL—compounding errors under visual distractions—and demonstrates backbone-agnostic benefits by also improving DreamerV3. It could enable more robust real-world deployment of vision-based RL agents without requiring data from unseen environments. VIGOR combines three interdependent components: asymmetric weak-to-strong augmentation, dynamics-level consistency via direct latent regression, and encoder-level stabilization. Ablations show the method is augmentation-agnostic, with robustness driven by latent-space consistency rather than the specific augmentation family used.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) learns a latent dynamics model from pixels and plans in that latent space, achieving high sample efficiency. However, visual distractions such as background or lighting changes can push encoder outputs out of distribution, and these errors compound during recursive latent rollouts, unlike in model-free RL where perturbations affect only single-step predictions. Zero-shot visual generalization aims to maintain performance under unseen visual conditions without fine-tuning.

## [ValuSpec: Plug-and-Play Candidate Valuation for Tree-Based Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31338) ⭐️ 6.0/10

ValuSpec introduces a training-free, plug-and-play candidate valuation mechanism that filters low-quality branches from speculative decoding candidate trees before target model verification, using a threshold estimated from the target model's greedy trajectories on a calibration split. Experiments on HumanEval, GSM8K, Dolly, and SpecBench show up to 2.38x end-to-end speedup in retrieval-based tree settings and up to 2.78x when integrated into the EAGLE-3 dynamic tree framework. This addresses a practical inefficiency in LLM serving: retrieval-based speculative decoding often produces candidate trees with many low-quality branches that waste expensive target-side computation. By filtering these branches without retraining or modifying tree construction, ValuSpec can be dropped into existing serving systems to improve throughput. ValuSpec uses transfer-based supervision to align filtering with the target model's behavior and guarantees that accepted tokens exactly match standard autoregressive decoding under the output-identical greedy rule. The threshold is estimated without any parameter training, making the method lightweight and compatible with existing tree construction strategies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by using a smaller draft model to propose multiple candidate tokens, which the larger target model then verifies in parallel. Tree-based speculative decoding extends this by verifying multiple candidate branches simultaneously, and retrieval-based methods reuse historical drafts to construct candidate trees for similar requests. However, as candidate trees grow, they often contain many low-quality branches that reduce verification efficiency.

## [DIO/ODI-Informed Authorization Guardrail for Agentic Entity Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37407) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes a DIO/ODI-informed authorization boundary that separates evidence used for reasoning from evidence eligible to authorize persistent identity-changing mutations in agentic entity resolution systems. It introduces a domain-governed constraint-binding function and an abstract two-phase supervisor that permits commitment only when claim-specific authorization-eligible evidence supports every required proposition, conflicts are resolved, scope is valid, and bound state and policy versions remain current. As agentic entity resolution systems increasingly propose and commit identity graph mutations autonomously, an explicit authorization contract could reduce the risk of erroneous identity hypotheses being confirmed through hypothesis-conditioned retrieval. This work is relevant to practitioners designing guardrails for autonomous data integration and digital identity management. The guarantee is conditional on adequate domain policy, sound claim-specific evidence promotion, and faithful enforcement; the specification does not independently establish promotion correctness or semantic truth. This is a conceptual architecture without implementation or empirical validation, and it does not prevent erroneous hypotheses or hypothesis-conditioned retrieval, leaving dependency-aware evidence flow as future research.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Agentic entity resolution systems iteratively plan actions, retrieve evidence, revise hypotheses, and propose graph mutations to match identities across data sources. Digital Identity Optimization (DIO) and the Ontology of Digital Identity (ODI) provide a framework for examining how identities become reconstructable, trusted, and readable across search systems, knowledge graphs, and AI-mediated environments.

## [TSBPO: Tree-Structured Branching for Policy Optimization in Interactive Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41697) ⭐️ 6.0/10

TSBPO introduces a tree-structured branching budget allocator and a tree-aware step-level credit assignment scheme to improve policy optimization for interactive language agents. Using a Qwen2.5-1.5B actor, it achieves 97.79% seen and 96.64% unseen stochastic success on ALFWorld, and 81.07% per-episode success over six stochastic episodes for each of 500 WebShop test goals. This work addresses two underdetermined problems in multi-turn agent training—rollout allocation and local credit assignment—by combining predictive sampling with tree-structured branching. It offers a practical method for improving sample efficiency and training stability in reinforcement learning for LLM agents, which could benefit researchers and practitioners building interactive agents. The allocator distributes factual rollouts across tasks and additional suffixes across replayable states using likelihood-fitted estimates of terminal-outcome contrast, while the credit assignment compares immediate sibling segments on the resulting physical rollout trees. Ablating the observation-group step term at the WebShop update-100 checkpoint reduces success to 75.93%, and the results are limited to the evaluated training configurations on two interactive benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning fine-tuning of large language models often relies on terminal rewards, which makes it difficult to determine which intermediate turns deserve credit and where to spend costly rollouts. Dynamics-Predictive Sampling (DPS) provides a task-level predictive sampling reference, but interactive agents also need to choose which visited states merit further continuation. Tree-structured rollout methods organize exploration by branching from informative intermediate states, and step-level credit assignment aims to assign outcome contrasts to the turns that produced them.

## [Hybrid UNet-SLIC Pipeline Boosts Fundus Lesion Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25549) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2509.25549v4) proposes a hybrid pipeline that combines a coarse UNet prior with training-free SLIC superpixels to segment choroidal nevi in fundus images at full resolution. On 25 held-out 1024×1024 images, the method achieved a mean Dice of 90.9%, outperforming nnU-Net (83.2%), Swin UNet (73.0%), and Attention UNet (60.6%). This approach addresses the challenge of delineating small, indistinct lesions at native resolution without requiring GPU inference, making it practical for clinical deployment on standard laptops. It offers a lightweight alternative to large deep learning models for medical image segmentation, potentially improving longitudinal monitoring of choroidal nevi. The UNet is trained on downsampled 128×128 images to provide a location prior, while SLIC recovers boundaries at full resolution; the prior also automatically selects the superpixel count via an image-to-lesion ratio, eliminating manual tuning. Inference takes 1.6 seconds per 1024×1024 image and 15 seconds at 3900×3900 on a laptop, and the advantage persisted on 90 external images from a second centre (76.8% vs. 69.3% Dice for nnU-Net).

**Background**: Choroidal nevi are common benign pigmented lesions in the eye that require monitoring because a small fraction can transform into melanoma. Accurate segmentation of these lesions in fundus images is difficult due to their small size (often <2% of the image), indistinct boundaries, and limited expert annotations. SLIC (Simple Linear Iterative Clustering) is a training-free superpixel algorithm that groups pixels into perceptually meaningful regions, while nnU-Net is a self-configuring deep learning framework that automatically adapts its pipeline to medical segmentation tasks.

The paper introduces Adaptive Diversity Cache (ADC), a training-free, plug-and-play framework that adaptively allocates test-time memory capacity based on class frequency to improve long-tailed human-object interaction (HOI) detection. Experiments on HICO-DET and V-COCO show that ADC improves mean average precision (mAP) for rare interactions by 4.02–8.57 percentage points and also improves non-rare performance. This work addresses the long-tailed distribution problem in HOI detection, where rare interactions are underrepresented in training data, by leveraging test-time observations without retraining the detector. The plug-and-play nature means it can complement existing HOI detectors, potentially benefiting real-world applications like surveillance and robotics that require recognizing uncommon interactions. ADC uses frequency-aware cache adaptation with a weighted logarithmic allocation objective to assign larger capacities to underrepresented interactions, and a confidence-diversity joint selection to retain informative cache entries. Online adaptation further achieves gains of 2.87–7.36 percentage points on rare interactions, and the method is evaluated across three base detectors.

Long-tailed learning addresses scenarios where some classes (rare interactions) have far fewer training examples than others, leading to poor performance on those classes. Cache-based test-time adaptation methods store features from test data to refine predictions without updating model weights, but typically allocate uniform memory across classes.

The paper introduces MOO, a large-scale synthetic aerial-ground cattle re-identification dataset containing 1,000 cattle individuals captured from 128 uniformly sampled viewpoints, totaling 128,000 annotated images. Using this controlled dataset, the authors quantify the influence of elevation and identify a critical elevation threshold above which models generalize significantly better to unseen views. This work addresses the lack of precise angular annotations in existing animal re-identification datasets, enabling systematic analysis of viewpoint variations in aerial-ground settings. The validated transfer to four real-world cattle datasets demonstrates that synthetic geometric priors can effectively bridge the domain gap, laying a foundation for future cross-view animal re-identification model development. The dataset is fully synthetic and publicly available on GitHub, with 128 uniformly sampled viewpoints providing precise angular annotations. The identified elevation threshold offers a concrete guideline for when models are likely to generalize better to unseen views, though the synthetic nature may introduce domain-specific biases.

## [BALD-SAM: Bayesian Active Prompting for Interactive Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10828) ⭐️ 6.0/10

BALD-SAM introduces a disagreement-based active prompting framework that uses Bayesian Active Learning by Disagreement (BALD) to select the most informative regions for annotator prompts in interactive segmentation with the Segment Anything Model (SAM). The method freezes the entire SAM model and applies Bayesian uncertainty modeling only to a small learned prediction head, making uncertainty estimation practical for large foundation models. This work addresses a practical annotation workflow problem by automating the selection of prompt locations, potentially reducing annotator effort and improving segmentation quality. It demonstrates strong cross-domain performance, ranking first or second on 14 of 16 benchmarks, and even surpassing human prompting in some cases. BALD-SAM was evaluated on 16 datasets spanning natural, medical, underwater, and seismic domains, with a comprehensive ablation suite covering 3 SAM backbones and 35 Laplace posterior configurations (38 distinct settings). It consistently outperforms one-shot baselines in final segmentation quality, particularly on thin and structurally complex objects.

**Background**: Interactive segmentation with SAM allows users to guide the model by placing prompts (e.g., clicks) to refine masks iteratively. Active learning is a paradigm where the model selects the most informative data points for labeling, and BALD is an information-theoretic acquisition function that quantifies epistemic uncertainty by measuring disagreement among model predictions. This work bridges active learning and interactive segmentation by treating spatial locations as an unlabeled pool and prompts as queries.

## [Burstiness Goodness Boosts Forward-Forward Algorithm Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13081) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper systematically studies selectivity and shape in Forward-Forward goodness functions, proposing burstiness goodness—a parameter-free, scale-invariant excess-kurtosis objective—and selective goodness functions based on top-k and entmax-weighted energy. On Fashion-MNIST, burstiness improves accuracy from 73.2±0.9% to 88.6±0.4% over sum-of-squares, and on CIFAR-10 it raises block-local ResNet-18 accuracy from 28.9±0.5% to 46.8±2.5%. This work shows that the choice of activity statistic in Forward-Forward learning significantly impacts performance, offering a simple yet effective alternative to standard sum-of-squares goodness. It could make local learning algorithms like FF more competitive on small-image tasks, potentially advancing biologically plausible deep learning research. The burstiness goodness rewards heavy-tailed activity patterns via excess kurtosis, and the study uses controlled experiments across six small-image datasets with validation-tuned, five-seed runs. However, the evaluation is limited to small images and block-local ResNet-18, so scalability to larger datasets and deeper networks remains untested.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: The Forward-Forward (FF) algorithm, introduced by Geoffrey Hinton in 2022, is a biologically plausible alternative to backpropagation that trains networks layer-by-layer using two forward passes (positive and negative data) and a local 'goodness' function. The goodness function measures how well a layer's activations respond to positive versus negative data, and its design is crucial for learning. Common choices include the sum of squared activations, but this paper explores more sophisticated statistics like kurtosis and selective energy.

## [EcoGEO reframes generative engine optimization as environment-level influence for LLM agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12887) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.12887v3) introduces EcoGEO, which reframes Generative Engine Optimization (GEO) as an environment-level influence problem for web-enabled LLM agents, and proposes TRACE, a Trajectory-Aware Coordinated Evidence Ecosystem. TRACE builds a controlled evidence environment with an agent-facing navigation entry page and heterogeneous support pages that use shared terminology, internal links, and consistent product attributes to introduce, verify, and reinforce a target product. This work shifts GEO from optimizing individual webpages to designing interconnected evidence ecosystems that guide an agent's multi-step browsing trajectory, which could change how brands and content creators influence AI-generated recommendations. It matters for anyone interested in the evolving relationship between web content and LLM-based search agents. The method is evaluated on OPR-Bench, a benchmark for open-ended product recommendation, and consistently outperforms page-level GEO baselines in Recommendation; trajectory-level metrics show improved initial target access and additional internal-link browsing, but follow-up-search effects vary across backbones and comparison methods. The paper is an arXiv preprint with no community discussion or peer review validation yet, and the abstract is truncated, limiting assessment of its impact.

## [VACE: Geometry-Controlled Self-Supervised Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23504) ⭐️ 6.0/10

VACE (Velocity-Aligned Channel Embeddings) is a self-supervised anomaly detection method that learns a locally smooth, directionally coherent latent representation of normality for multivariate time series, trained with a velocity-consistency objective using no negatives and no synthetic anomalies. At test time it combines a Mahalanobis positional score with a directional score computed against a bank of normal directions, achieving state-of-the-art results on TSB-AD-M. Contrastive approaches shape the latent space only indirectly through pair-sampling heuristics, leaving the geometry that distance-based scoring relies on to chance; VACE shows that explicitly controlling this geometry yields better detection with a simpler, cheaper training scheme. This matters for practitioners in domains like industrial monitoring and IT operations, where labels are scarce and missed anomalies are costly. The method uses a channel-aware encoder trained with a velocity-consistency objective, and its directional score acts multiplicatively as a gate that amplifies the positional score where local dynamics are atypical. The authors report that VACE outperforms more complex methods trained with substantially larger budgets, though the abstract does not detail computational cost or sensitivity to hyperparameters.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Anomaly detection in multivariate time series is hard because anomalies are rare, labels are usually unavailable, and a miss can be expensive. Self-supervised representation learning addresses this by embedding temporal patches into a latent space where normal behavior occupies a well-defined region, so anomalies can be flagged by geometric deviation. Contrastive learning is the dominant approach, but it relies on sampling positive and negative pairs to shape that space indirectly. VACE instead directly enforces a smooth, directionally coherent geometry for normal trajectories.

## [CTS-MoE: Jointly Trained Perception-Gated Experts for Smooth Legged Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19633) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces CTS-MoE, a method that jointly trains a perception-gated dense mixture-of-experts actor with task-specific critics for perceptive legged locomotion. It achieves 93.3% success on a connected course, reduces mean action jerk by about a third, and completes an extended outdoor course in 10/10 trials on a Unitree Go1. This work addresses the challenge of smooth transitions between different terrains in legged locomotion, which is critical for real-world deployment. The joint training approach avoids the switching transients of hierarchical selectors and the information loss of distillation, potentially enabling more robust and agile robots. The method uses soft routing to compose expert knowledge into continuously varying behavior, and separated critics to isolate heterogeneous objectives. A concurrent teacher-student formulation transfers the composition from privileged terrain information to onboard depth, so routing is inferred from perception alone without terrain labels or a selector at deployment.

Mixture-of-experts (MoE) is a neural network architecture where multiple specialized sub-networks (experts) are combined by a gating mechanism, allowing the model to handle diverse tasks. Teacher-student distillation is a technique where a privileged teacher policy (with access to extra information during training) trains a student policy that relies only on deployable sensors.

## [TraceViT adds grounded trace supervision to looped visual reasoners for ARC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29586) ⭐️ 6.0/10

TraceViT is a looped visual reasoner trained on semantically monotonic transformation chains, obtained by rewriting and verifying programmatic ARC task implementations to decompose each solution into intermediate grid states. It uses soft trace alignment to enforce only the ordering of these chains, letting the model allocate iterations freely, and achieves 67.8% pass@2 on ARC-AGI-1 and 24.3% on ARC-AGI-2. Conventional training of looped reasoners constrains only the final output, leaving intermediate refinements unconstrained; TraceViT shows that supervising these intermediate steps can improve abstract reasoning performance on the widely used ARC benchmark. This could inform future work on iterative refinement and program synthesis for out-of-distribution generalization. Controlled ablations on ARC-AGI-1 show that trace supervision becomes beneficial only when paired with grounding, which provides each iteration with a task reference derived from few-shot demonstrations and an object workspace representing the current grid state. Code and data are promised at the project's GitHub repository.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) is a visual program synthesis benchmark that tests whether a model can infer an unseen transformation from a few input-output examples and apply it to a new grid, emphasizing out-of-distribution generalization. Looped visual reasoners refine predictions over multiple iterations by reusing parameters, but standard training only constrains the final output. TraceViT introduces intermediate supervision by decomposing verified solutions into step-by-step grid states and aligning them softly to the model's iterations.

## [Locally Tokenized VQ-VAE Model Stabilizes Time-Series Watermark Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19727) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper diagnoses bidirectional drift in globally coupled re-encoding detectors for multivariate time-series watermarking and proposes L-VQVAE, a generative model where each discrete token is produced from a short contiguous window, along with LVQMark, a watermarking method combining logit-bias injection with robust re-encoding. Experiments on four benchmarks spanning finance, energy, and neuroimaging show stabilized detection power and false-positive behavior under post-editing attacks while preserving generation quality. Reliable watermarking is essential for provenance in generative models, and this work addresses a fundamental reliability failure that invalidates clean-calibrated thresholds under post-editing attacks. The locality principle could influence the design of future watermarking schemes for time-series and other structured data, benefiting researchers and practitioners concerned with model attribution. The key insight is that reliable detection requires each recovered unit to depend only on a bounded temporal neighborhood, motivating the locally tokenized L-VQVAE architecture. The method is evaluated on four benchmarks from finance, energy, and neuroimaging, though the abstract does not specify the exact attack types or quantitative improvements.

**Background**: Vector-quantized variational autoencoders (VQ-VAEs) learn discrete latent representations by mapping continuous encoder outputs to the nearest vectors in a codebook, enabling generative modeling over discrete tokens. Watermarking for generative models typically embeds a statistical signal and detects it via a z-score under a null hypothesis; in time-series settings, post-editing attacks can distort the null distribution, causing detection failures.

## [BROT: Deep Barycentric Regression Achieves Minimax Optimal OT Map Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06598) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces BROT (Barycentric Regression for Optimal Transport), a two-step method that first computes the unregularized OT plan and then fits a deep neural network to the induced barycentric targets via least-squares regression. Under standard regularity conditions, the authors prove that the DNN estimator attains the minimax convergence rate when the ground-truth OT map is Lipschitz. This work bridges the gap between theoretically optimal but impractical nonparametric OT map estimators and scalable parametric estimators lacking statistical guarantees, offering a simple method with both practical scalability and minimax optimality. It could benefit machine learning applications such as single-cell perturbation prediction and unsupervised domain adaptation, where accurate OT map estimation translates to stronger task performance. The minimax optimality guarantee relies on standard regularity conditions and assumes the ground-truth OT map is Lipschitz; numerical studies on synthetic and image datasets show accurate map estimates, strong target distribution matching, and competitive transport costs compared to existing methods. The two-step approach avoids the sensitive min-max adversarial-like training objectives of existing parametric estimators by using least-squares regression on barycentric targets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Optimal transport (OT) provides a geometric way to align probability distributions by finding a map that transports one distribution to another with minimal cost, and it has become a popular tool in machine learning. Estimating the OT map from finite samples is challenging: nonparametric estimators can achieve minimax optimal convergence rates but are often computationally demanding, while parametric deep-learning estimators are scalable but lack sharp statistical guarantees and rely on unstable adversarial training. Minimax convergence rate is a standard notion in nonparametric statistics that measures the best possible worst-case convergence speed of an estimator.

## [DynSHAP: Explainable Dynamic Survival Analysis with Time-Feature Shapley Players](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13042) ⭐️ 6.0/10

DynSHAP proposes a SHAP-based explainability framework for dynamic survival analysis that treats time-feature pairs as players in the Shapley game, and introduces Temporal DynSHAP, which learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to address them. On synthetic data with known ground-truth attributions, Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent features more accurately than marginal estimators for a given state-of-the-art model. Deep learning models for dynamic survival analysis achieve strong predictive performance but remain black boxes, limiting clinical trust and adoption; existing explainability methods cannot handle longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs simultaneously. DynSHAP fills this gap and, when applied to two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures, produces attributions faithful to model learning, allowing medical experts to see which patient information drove the prediction and when. The framework extends common marginal SHAP estimators to the DSA setting by treating time-feature pairs as players, while Temporal DynSHAP learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to account for them. Validation relies on synthetic data with known ground-truth attributions plus two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures, but the work is a niche methodological advance without broad community discussion or immediate industry impact.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) is a game-theoretic approach that explains model outputs by assigning each input feature a contribution based on Shapley values from cooperative game theory. Dynamic survival analysis (DSA) uses longitudinal patient data, such as repeated measurements over time, to predict survival outcomes dynamically, and deep learning models for DSA are accurate but opaque. Standard SHAP assumes independent or static features and cannot directly handle irregular longitudinal inputs or functional survival outputs, which is the gap DynSHAP targets.

## [Logged Effective Dimension in Hybrid QNNs Measures Circuit Geometry, Not Model Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14070) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.14070v2) proves that the effective dimension commonly logged in hybrid quantum neural networks is actually the participation ratio of Pauli-Z output sensitivities, not the quantum Fisher information matrix (QFIM) as often assumed. Through experiments on CIFAR-10 and EuroSAT across 4–16 qubits, 1–4 layers, three entangling patterns, and three seeds, the authors show this scalar stays within a narrow range (6.63–9.13) even as accuracy varies from 23.14% to 86.44%. This work warns quantum machine learning practitioners that a widely logged capacity metric does not track model quality, so capacity, trainability, and accuracy each require separate diagnostics. It could lead to more rigorous evaluation practices and prevent misleading conclusions in hybrid QNN research. The paper shows each Pauli-Z sensitivity is at most n times its QFIM diagonal entry on n qubits, and that a participation ratio of the QFIM diagonal overstates the full-matrix PR by an exact factor determined by off-diagonal mass. It also finds that depth raises the share of active parameters from 0.39 to 0.91, initial gradient variance falls by a factor of 34.5 from 4 to 16 qubits, and removing entanglement improves accuracy by 5.3 to 8.2 points at the same parameter count.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Hybrid quantum neural networks combine a classical feature extractor with a variational quantum circuit, and researchers often log a scalar called the effective dimension as a proxy for model capacity. The effective dimension is typically defined as the participation ratio of the quantum Fisher information matrix (QFIM), which characterizes parameter sensitivity, but in practice a cheaper participation ratio of parameter sensitivities is computed instead. This paper clarifies what that cheap scalar actually measures and tests its relationship to performance.

## [Damped Trust-Region Method Speeds Up Dynamic k-NN Graph Construction in Equivariant Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19246) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces a two-pass scheduling method with damped trust-region compensation to approximate dynamic k-NN graph construction in SE(3)-equivariant structure prediction, achieving a 1.76x epoch speed-up with no measurable loss gap. It provides theoretical guarantees including exact non-flip certificates, minimum-variance replay gain, and an analytic near-tie spacing law. This work addresses a key efficiency bottleneck in equivariant structure prediction pipelines, potentially enabling faster training and inference for applications like protein structure prediction. The theoretical framework and open-source implementation could inspire similar optimizations in other graph-based equivariant models. The method implements three compensation levels in C++: L1 (frozen set with live geometry), L2 (margin-gated local repair), and L3 (damped, projected basis). L2 exactly reproduces the per-boundary edge set (D=0.0000), while L1 stays within 0.6% and L3 interpolates monotonically; the approach is validated under a matched-conditions protocol and the code is available on GitHub.

Researchers propose ACSAN, an all-convolutional self-attention network that replaces fully-connected matrix multiplications in Vision Transformers with convolutional operations. The model introduces Dynamic Convolutional Self-Attention (DCSA) using multi-scale dilated convolutions to generate Query, Key, and Value, and replaces MLPs with Equivalent Convolutional Fully Connected (EqConvFC) modules, achieving 93.52% on CIFAR-10, 73.38% on CIFAR-100, and 83.3% on ImageNet-1k with fewer parameters than standard ViT. The DCSA module computes spatial and channel self-attention separately using multi-scale dilated convolutions, then merges the two attention features. ACSAN is trained from scratch on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet-1k, but the paper is a single arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

## [Merge and Branch: Tree-Structured Fine-Tuning Boosts LLM Recommendation Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37973) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Merge and Branch (M&B), a tree-structured supervised fine-tuning method that compresses each historical item's semantic identifier (SID) and text into a few learned tokens, then uses the compressed history to predict multiple target SIDs. It also develops tree FlashAttention and a tree-structured KV cache to share history computation across branches during both training and constrained decoding. LLM-based generative recommendation suffers from an asymmetry where long interaction histories are expensive to encode but provide supervision for only one target, making fine-tuning inefficient. M&B addresses this by learning more from each encoded history, and online A/B tests on a large e-commerce platform show statistically significant gains in clicks, orders, and GMV, suggesting practical value for industrial recommender systems. The method organizes each example as a shallow, wide tree so that the shared history is processed once while gradients from every branch propagate end-to-end. A tree-structured key/value cache extends history sharing to constrained decoding, and experiments on public benchmarks show M&B outperforms strong baselines while substantially improving training and decoding efficiency.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Generative recommendation with LLMs typically represents items by semantic identifiers (SIDs), discrete token sequences that encode item semantics, and feeds long text-rich interaction histories into the model. Because encoding these histories is costly while each history usually supervises only one target SID, supervised fine-tuning is inefficient. FlashAttention is an IO-aware exact attention algorithm that reduces memory reads/writes between GPU HBM and SRAM via tiling, and KV caching stores past key/value states to speed up autoregressive decoding; M&B builds tree-structured variants of both to share history computation across multiple prediction branches.

<a id="item-374"></a>
## [Multi-Estimate Elicitation Fails to Uniformly Debias LLM Numerical Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00597) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A large preregistered study (22,212 units across six frozen configurations) tested whether eliciting five weighted estimates within a single response reduces cognitive biases in LLM numerical judgments across anchoring, hindsight, base-rate, conjunction, and status-quo/default tasks. Of 45 confirmatory tests, only one adjusted effect exceeded its smallest effect size of interest: gpt-oss-120b showed a 10.68-percentage-point increase in post-outcome confirmation error under multi-estimate elicitation. This finding challenges the common assumption that multi-estimate elicitation uniformly debiases LLM judgments, suggesting that prompting strategies for debiasing may be model- and task-specific. It has implications for AI/ML researchers designing evaluation protocols and for practitioners relying on LLM numerical judgments in high-stakes settings. Only 12 of 45 tests rejected on complete cases, and 11 retained directional confirmation after a missingness-protection rule; the remaining 10 retained effects had sub-threshold point estimates, and non-rejection does not establish equivalence. Joint presentation lowered signed conjunction excess in four configurations but confounded constraint visibility and call structure, and post-hoc candidate-spread associations remain in-sample and require independent validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Multi-estimate elicitation is a prompting technique where a language model is asked to produce several weighted estimates within one response, with the hope that aggregating them reduces cognitive biases such as anchoring or hindsight. Cognitive biases are systematic patterns of deviation from norm or rationality in judgment, and they can affect LLM outputs in ways that mirror human biases. The smallest effect size of interest (SESOI) is the smallest effect that would be considered theoretically or practically meaningful, and preregistration involves specifying hypotheses and analysis plans before data collection to increase rigor.

<a id="item-375"></a>
## [Risk-constrained evaluation framework for financial document recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02775) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes a human-centred, risk-constrained evaluation framework for financial document recognition that treats recognition as evidence recovery and decision support, using non-compensatory acceptance gates for critical-field errors. It introduces layered quality models, selective automation as constrained routing, and formal results on the limitations of compensatory scoring. Current metrics like character and word error rates fail to capture whether systems preserve critical financial facts, so this framework could improve trust and safety in automated financial document processing. It affects developers, auditors, and regulators who rely on accurate extraction of amounts, identifiers, and dates. The framework uses one-sided uncertainty bounds for critical-field errors, preventing strong aggregate scores from masking failures in amounts, identifiers, signs, periods, or entity associations. It also specifies annotation procedures, leakage-resistant benchmark partitions, and a reproducibility card, but is theoretical with no new model, dataset, or empirical results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 6, 05:00

**Background**: Financial document recognition systems are often evaluated using character and word error rates, which measure transcription accuracy but not whether critical financial facts are correctly preserved. Non-compensatory acceptance gates are thresholds that cannot be offset by strong performance elsewhere, ensuring that critical errors are not masked by high average scores. Selective automation routes uncertain cases to human review to manage residual risk.
